#6828. Designers Say These Are the Best Colors to Pair With Blue in Any Room

They perfectly complement the rainbow's most versatile hue.

A designer-favorite color to use in the home is beautiful, calming blue. Evoking natural elements like the sea and the sky, this serene hue can make any room feel more relaxed and welcoming, yet also more vibrant and bold, depending on the shades. And if this versatile hue is one you’re thinking about adding to your home, check out these beautiful colors that go with blue, proven by these designer spaces.

No matter your design style, this list of 38 colors that go with blue serves as evidence that the hue is as versatile as a neutral and can elevate any space you use it in. Read on to find your new favorite color scheme here.

To contrast the fanciful chinoiserie wallpaper in this San Francisco apartment, interior designer Caitlin Jones Ghajar and her team went bold and chose a warm yellow for the velvet bed frame. “This particular kind of blue lends itself really well to the vistas that are beyond, so you get some Bay view looks,” she says.

In this Amber Lewis–designed kitchen, the duck egg blue cabinetry is grounded by the rust red and cream floor tile. The hue, Light Blue by Farrow & Ball, can lean almost gray in soft lighting, making it a versatile neutral ready to pair with warm tones.

The deep, black accents in this kitchen add drama and depth to the slate blue cabinetry. The graphic color pairing is modern and masculine, while not trying to compete with the lush nature outdoors. Melissa Anderson of OAD Interiors designed this immersive space in Woodstock, New York.

Blue and yellow is a classic and cheerful color pairing. Elevate the duo by choosing a hue with a slightly greener tone, like a chartreuse. In this guest room by French & French, the cool light blue prevents a too-neon appearance.

Pairing a cool tone with a warm tone is a failsafe approach, and this bedroom by Serena Dugan does it expertly. A golden tan wallpaper softens the vibrant indigo accents on the headboard and window treatments. If you’re feeling bold, throw in some orange for added impact.

When you're unsure of what colors go with blue the best, simply opt for more of the same, as French & French did here. Perfect pattern play is achieved in this lounge through the matching blue and white palettes. A cobalt blue wall ties it all together.

For an impactful palette, add some glitz to your shade of blue. This living room, designed by Suzanne Kasler, utilizes metallics like brass and bronze to elevate the everyday hue to glamorous new heights.

In this Santa Fe home designed by the duo behind French & French, a rich, terra-cotta red balances the vibrant blue tones. To ground the swirling marbled wallpaper on the ceiling, the grand bookcases and coordinating rug (and sneaky chair piping!) incorporate the hue throughout the entire space.

In House Beautiful’s 2024 Whole Home, the guest room is cocooned in a mustard and navy color palette. Blue and yellow primary colors play off one another for a vibrant interior. Use neutral bedding to ground the bright shades.

Pink and blue can often feel like a baby shower or gender reveal, but take inspiration from designer Dane Austin to know how to use them correctly. By combining a pastel with a more vibrant tone, you can elevate the color pairing from juvenile to sophisticated.

Look to nature for hues to use with blue. This chic powder room, complete with a navy blue sink, is swathed in a floral green wallpaper. This color pairing is cheerful and can brighten up a smaller space, especially this one by Liz Carroll, which lacks windows.

Adorned in shades of cobalt and navy, the primary bedroom in our 2024 Whole Home in Asheville, North Carolina, continues the moody color palette with an ochre wallpaper. The hues are simultaneously subdued yet impactful, ideal for a serene night’s sleep.

A spa-like and timeless blue and gray color palette can brighten even the dingiest basements. To ensure nothing looks out of place, keep both hues at the same vibrancy level (e.g., a light blue and a light gray).

To add drama to this teal bedroom, designer Lisa Tharp opted for a totally red upholstered bed frame, and somehow it works. Blue and red are both primary colors, which means they pair well when one starts to lean toward the other’s complement (like the slightly green-toned blue walls).

In this playful kids' bathroom, a bright blue is the focal point color in the space. Looking to the color wheel for a clue, homeowner Ashley Maddox and designer Hilary Walker opted for a burnt orange shower curtain as the accent hue.

Pairing a myriad of cool-toned colors can create a sophisticated palette when done well, like in this Boston apartment. A purple rug complements the blue sectional sofa because they have similar textiles, tones, and brightness levels.

If you’re a fan of the blue and yellow primary pair, you’ll love this elevated option. Pairing a deep, rich blue, like this navy kitchen, with a metallic brass accent is a great way to add some glamour and texture to your space.

Monochrome color palettes can be really impactful when done with various shades of the same hue. Take this living room by Mark D. Sikes for example. The deep blue upholstery on the sofa and ottoman ground the lighter blue used to color drench the walls and ceiling.

Avery Cox of Avery Cox Design used a tonal blue for the wall to give this room an immersive effect and paired it with pops of bubblegum pink, purple, and citron yellow. The colors are the perfect pick-me-up when you're lounging indoors on a rainy day.

This robin's egg blue wallpaper is speckled with ivory and forest green to make daydreaming easy. The ivory mirror and green accessories designer Shannon Eddings added to this dining room are the supporting characters that soften the blue so it doesn't overwhelm the space.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6827. Jennifer Lopez Is Covered in Crystals for a Surprise Fishnet-Clad Performance in NYC

Jennifer Lopez sure knows how to make an entrance. Last night, the Grammy-nominated singer appeared on the stage of New York City’s Metropolitan Museum of Art atop the shoulders of two dancers, and proceeded to deliver an intimate performance.

Merging classic Old Hollywood glamour with her quintessential pop-star look, the artist paired a slinky metallic dress and fishnet tights with a dramatic black fur stole and a crystal headpiece reminiscent of a 1920s flapper cap. As Lopez moved, the gown was revealed to have a corset bodice and gathered skirt, cinched with a bejeweled brooch.

Later, Lopez stripped down into a shimmering, skintight fishnet catsuit designed with a statement halter neckline and a barely-there nude leotard underneath. The built-in choker necklace was embellished with chunky crystals, which appeared to cascade down the catsuit’s bodice by trimming the hemline. The jewels were the perfect match to the singer’s long, dangly earrings, crafted from strands of crystal fringe that danced as she did. (They were also the perfect match to her bedazzled microphone.)

It was in this second look that the star debuted a new song, “Proof of Love,” which was accompanied by hits such as “On the Floor,” “Waiting for Tonight,” and “Love Don’t Cost a Thing.” Throughout it all, Lopez’s red-bottomed lace-up Christian Louboutin boots grounded her on-stage looks with a bit of edge, while her long wavy locks made for some theatrical hairography.

The performance took place during an event for financial technology company Bilt, which provides members with exclusive rewards and events (like surprise mini concerts).

#6825. Princess Kate Says Nature Helped Her Heal After Her Cancer Diagnosis

“People have long sought comfort in the natural world”

It’s not very often that Princess Catherine sits down for a traditional magazine interview, but in a new story published today in Country Life, the Princess of Wales got candid about her recovery following her 2024 cancer diagnosis and subsequent chemotherapy treatment (she went into remission early last year).

“I was told in hospital that my recovery would not be linear,” Kate says. “[That] there will be good days and bad days, that it will take time.”

She adds: “What is interesting is to see the science now back up what we instinctively know and feel. Humans have always turned to nature. Whether it is the beach for healing and recovery, the countryside for peace and perspective or the mountains to find solitude, people have long sought comfort in the natural world. It is such a restorative environment and incorporating nature into someone’s recovery pathway is vital.”

This past June, Kate completed the National Three Peaks Challenge, a feat that involves climbing the three highest peaks in the United Kingdom in a matter of only 24 hours. Part of the reason she took on the challenge was to highlight “the deeper impact of serious illness and the importance of holistic healthcare.”

The royal has also been teaming up with organizations that focus on outdoor activity and mental health, like Mind Over Mountains and the Dose of Nature charity.

In the wake of these events, interviewer Robert Macfarlane spoke with Kate about how nature tied to her recovery process. Of her “good days and bad days,” Kate noted that nature demonstrates the same ebb and flow. She said, “It shows us that periods of rest, reflection, and renewal are equally as important as moments of growth.”

Kate also spoke about her longstanding love of the outdoors. “I remember from a very early age spending a lot of time in nature, playing in streams, walking in the countryside, blackberry picking or simply time alone in my own world, enjoying the simplicity of ordinary things, such as digging under the laurel bush for what I thought was treasure,” she said. “There was always a sense of freedom I felt in nature, something about being outdoors that is hard to find elsewhere.”

She went on to note how, as adults, we often lose this sense of wonderment, and she hopes to instill the same fascination with the natural world in her three kids: Prince George, 13, Princess Charlotte, 11, and Prince Louis, 8. “We are often so busy we simply forget to pause, slow down and notice the detail in life,” Kate said. “It is something I remind myself to do with my own children; to give them freedom to explore, rather than constantly telling them what to see or what to think.”

The Hudson Valley inn lends its old-world touch to the brand.

There’s nothing quite like the holiday season to boost your sense of nostalgia for cozy moments at home—especially when they involve a touch of cold-weather whimsy. Whether you’re sipping on a hot coffee or tea as you watch the changing leaves or you’re curled up next to a warm fireplace with your latest read, the colder months of the year are primed for ample charm. Of course, these moments are not without their accoutrements, which is right where the new Madewell collaboration with The Six Bells—a country inn located in the Hudson Valley—comes in.

The Six Bells Inn, founded by Audrey Gelman, features interiors inspired by the imagined English village known as Barrow’s Green—complete with its own fictional characters and landmarks—giving the space an old-world touch that feels as if it’s been plucked right out of a storybook. It’s this distinct aesthetic, along with Gelman’s way of storytelling, that led to the inn’s collaboration with Madewell.

“Steven [Cateron, Madewell’s creative director,] and the team at Madewell understood our little world at Six Bells from the very first conversation we had. In the early stages of working together on this collaboration, we wanted this collection to feel like stepping through a doorway into another time where things feel a little more enchanted,” Gelman told ELLE.com in an email. “Every piece in the collection was designed to have that sense of wonder. We hope it brings the spirit of The Six Bells into people’s lives and homes this holiday season.”

#6818. 34 Unique Christmas Tree Themes That'll Inspire You This Holiday Season

From bejeweled ornaments to popcorn garlands, these ideas bring major holiday cheer.

When it comes to Christmas tree decor, some folks keep it classic, using the same ornaments every year, while others choose new themes. Both approaches capture the charm and spirit of the season. However, if you find yourself in the latter cohort, you know how hard it can be to find yearly inspiration that will wow your guests and blend with your existing decor. So, if you’re in need of a muse, we’ve curated a list of tree themes so stunning, you’ll want to repeat them next year.

While many people stick to the traditional charm of classic hues, this year offers bold alternatives. From glittering jeweled ornaments to vintage-inspired touches like tinsel, popcorn garlands, and flocked branches, social feeds are buzzing with fresh, trendy ideas and new possibilities. There's a lot of Christmas creativity to spread the cheer this year.

To spark your imagination, we've compiled 34 merry and bright Christmas tree themes that are sure to bring jolly spirits. Whether you're drawn to the rustic farmhouse aesthetic or punchy plum color trends, these seasonal styles will infuse your home with magic and joy—and as a bonus, they look fantastic on both real and faux firs.

A Christmas tree can quickly become crowded, so take inspiration from Anthropologie and Terrain's holiday house and swap your standard ornaments for oversized baubles. These larger, multicolored orbs make a true statement, especially when paired with vibrant lights or flocked branches.

Enjoying a warm drink after a long day of skiing was the inspiration for this miniature tree. Featuring gloves, boots, and a Santa with his own pair of skis, take inspiration and decorate a tree dedicated to your favorite seasonal hobby.

For those who like their ornaments to be matchy-matchy, it might be time to start collecting. Anthropologie displayed an adorable miniature tree decked out with festive mice. It's accompanied by a village fit for their Christmas-ready friends.

Ideal for the girly-girl in your life, or maybe even a coastal holiday setting, think outside of the box and abandon the evergreen altogether this year. Instead, take inspiration from design firm BEFoundCollective's white Christmas tree, decorated with cheerful pastel ornaments.

There are few things cozier than a flocked Christmas tree. This one by Pottery Barn is adorned with cozy and metallic ornaments like stars, birds, and even pinecones. This Christmas tree theme is great for a mountain or woodsy cabin, but you can even bring that countryside Christmas energy into your city apartment.

A soothing yet vibrant shade, you can't go wrong with a Christmas tree decked out in peach and orange tones. The silver tinsel contrasts perfectly while adding some glitz and glam. This tree, by the design firm BEFoundCollective in Virginia, showcases vintage ornaments among the lush foliage.

Interior designer Beata Heuman ties dozens of bows on the branches of her showroom's Christmas tree for easy and low-cost DIY decorations. Use ribbons in the traditional Christmas color scheme, match your interior's decor, or, like Heuman, choose hues from the entire rainbow.

Perfect for warmer climates (or those wishing spring would circle back around already), forgo traditional ornaments and adorn your tree with faux florals. Take inspiration from the Winterthur Museum, Garden, and Library in Delaware and use more voluminous blooms like hydrangeas as filler between smaller flowers like mums or daffodils.

A classic color scheme beloved by modern, vintage, and even Art Deco lovers, a black and gold palette is timeless. Make your tree stand tall with a bold trunk cover and top with a snowflake-inspired star to tie your entire decor scheme together.

Topped with a cheerful snowman, this Christmas tree theme is all about the seasonal icons we've come to love. Gnomes and mini Santas stand out against the lush foliage of the tree while striped baubles and twinkling stars glam up the traditional red-and-white color palette.

If your little one is still running around singing "Part of Your World" from The Little Mermaid, pay tribute to their favorite princess with a sophisticated Christmas tree theme. Chock-full of glittering mermaids, chic metal whales, and glossy sea turtles, this tree designed by Pottery Barn is the perfect way to bring the film to life for the holidays.

While winter whites always get the bulk of the attention, blue is the other color of the season we're suggesting you embrace. Opt for chinoiserie-style ornaments and a decadent velvet ribbon garland to make it feel just as cozy as red and green.

Perfect for the diva of the family, this Anthropologie-designed Christmas tree is all about glamorous, textured accents like peacock feathers, Depression-style glass, and velvet accents. Christmas is allowed to be over-the-top!

The summer of Barbie may be over, but that joyous love of all things pink can (and should) continue all year long. If you're feeling as bold as Margot Robbie's iconic character, go all in on a bright pink tree with gold and white accents. Like Barbie, your Christmas tree can be anything you want it to.

If you're always looking at moon phases and tarot cards, bring that love of all things astrological to your Christmas decorations this year. Golden crescent moons, flying doves, and iron-star-studded wreaths are incorporated in this flocked tree. Add warm-hued string lights, and the decorations take on an ethereal glow.

Classics are classics for a reason—and nothing is more instantly festive than a hallmark red-and-white Christmas tree. This bountiful beauty was decked by Inspired by Charm, who included lodge-inspired elements like buffalo check wrapping paper and wooden skis to make it feel fresh and fanciful.

If a streamlined and sleek aesthetic is more your style, use this Christmas tree by For the Home as inspiration. The intentionally sparse look nods to traditional Scandinavian spruces, which feature less dense branches and are left mostly bare save for a few basic ball ornaments and twinkling white lights.

Why choose just two colors when you can have all of them? This Technicolor tree created by Serretta Style leans into a more-is-more aesthetic with tiers of ornaments in every color of the rainbow. While decorations in the same shapes and designs are used here (just in different shades), you could get the same effect by hanging your existing ornaments by color from top to bottom.

The weather outside may be frightful, but it certainly inspires a delightful Christmas tree design! Nod to all things snow and ice with a winter-wonderland-themed evergreen. This scene designed by Lolly Jane combines a flocked tree with an assortment of white and silver ornaments—like plush "snowballs" and frosty pinecones—for a snowy and chic scene. Plenty of warm white lights and a pair of ice skates complete the charming look.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

On top of being the magazine's go-to Waco expert, Meghan also touches on quite a few other verticals in the digital space, like hot news content featuring your favorite HGTV stars, home design inspiration, and the latest interior TikTok trends. However, her favorite pieces are the ones that start conversations, such as stories about resale value and whether or not the Solo cup is chic. Before becoming a home writer, Meghan worked in the fashion industry for two years, writing for Harper's Bazaar after graduating from Iowa State University with a bachelor's degree in apparel merchandising. When she's not interviewing interior designers about home trends, you can find Meghan reading a fictional thriller with her cat in her lap, planning themed parties, or strolling through flea markets searching for chunky candle holders and vintage bookends.

#6817. Cartier Emergency? Inside the High-Stakes World of a Luxury Fashion Concierge

Private shopping appointments, last-minute deliveries, and the art of whatever the client wants.

Isn’t a fashion emergency fun when it happens to someone else? Think of the dress ripping in My Best Friend’s Wedding, or the frantic dash down Seventh Avenue in The Devil Wears Prada. These crises happen in real life, too, but are considerably less fun when they’re happening to us. How many times have you had an event and realized your look wasn’t quite right just minutes before the Uber arrives? Or stared into your suitcase on vacation wishing you had brought the espadrilles, not the sandals?

These are the sorts of requests handled by Quintessentially, the London-founded luxury concierge company that has spent the past 26 years building a business around the proposition that, for a certain class of consumer, almost anything can be arranged. Now operating in 40 cities, its U.S. operation spans private concierge services, travel, events, and guest management for luxury brands. Memberships start at $25,000 per year.

Its fashion services are particularly revealing: part cultural access and part tactical operation, run through a network of relationships built carefully over decades. Each member is assigned a lifestyle manager and a travel specialist, with a wider team of experts behind them.

“Our lifestyle managers are the lifeblood of Quintessentially,” says Lauren Wilt, U.S. CEO of Quintessentially. Many come from celebrity personal assisting, luxury hospitality, music, and entertainment—industries that reward discretion, speed, and an ability to handle big personalities. And learning the ins and outs of their clients’ idiosyncrasies is the key to their business.

“There isn’t another concierge company that gets to know their clients the way we do,” says Wilt. “This isn’t a self-service booking platform with a suite of benefits. We are a highly personalized, proactive service.”

Proactive being the operative word, and fashion offers plenty of opportunity for a lifestyle manager’s creative problem-solving. “Our members often come to us saying, ‘I’ve heard about this experience—hook me up and book it,’” said Wilt. “But it’s our job to tell them when to go, when to avoid the crowds, and sometimes that the experience they’ve seen on Instagram isn’t actually the experience for them.”

For one particularly devoted member, Quintessentially organized a Paris itinerary that was the stuff shopping dreams are made of. There were styling appointments at Dior ahead of a runway show and after-party, a private Chanel appointment arranged around a wish list submitted in advance, and lunch with Isabel Marant at her studio before attending her show. Hair and makeup were, of course, handled too. The itinerary also included a tour of vintage ateliers and heritage boutiques, with a specialist who negotiated purchases on the client’s behalf. Trop chic.

The service extends beyond the easily bookable, too. Another Quintessentially member was granted private access to Dior’s historic design archives and La Suite Dior, an invitation-only experience involving a private fragrance session, photo shoot, and a private after-hours visit to the Dior boutique. Other members have been invited into Bottega Veneta’s private New York residence, where rare and made-to-order pieces are presented alongside private dining and events.

But it’s not all about the shopping—a concierge service can also carefully curate once-in-a-lifetime experiences. When one member sought to propose to his girlfriend in Paris, Quintessentially arranged for a proposal on a flower-filled private balcony overlooking the Eiffel Tower. Afterward, the couple visited Cartier for a private appointment to choose her engagement ring together. And a surprise upgrade to the Karl Lagerfeld Suite at Hôtel de Crillon was secured.

The team also excels at solving logistical challenges. In one case, a diamond ring was collected in Geneva and delivered to a member in their suite at the Cheval Blanc Paris. And when a guest needed—truly needed—a new Saint Laurent bag and Bulgari necklace hand-delivered to their resort in the Maldives, Quintessentially made it happen.

“A brand sells you the dress; we help you with where to wear it,” Wilt says. Indeed, Quintessentially’s relationships with fashion houses operate in both directions. Luxury brands employ the company to manage the logistics surrounding their most important clients, as well as influencer trips and large-scale fashion shows and shopping events. One recent project for a fashion client involved bringing 100 couples to New York over four days, with Quintessentially coordinating car services, welcome lunches, and all the practical details that allowed the brand to focus on hosting.

But perhaps the most interesting thing about a service like Quintessentially is that it isn’t really about acquiring new things. It’s about the removal of friction, and the ability to move through the world with a degree of spontaneity. The real luxury isn’t the Birkin, the hotel suite, or the private fitting—it’s in never having to wonder how to make it all happen.

#6815. What Is an Upper Bleph? Here’s What to Know About the Eyelid Surgery

Doctors explain how the procedure works, when a brow lift may be needed, and nonsurgical options.

In the world of plastic surgery, the nose, breasts, and butt have commanded a lot of time and attention under the knife. But as tech advances and patients seek out more subtle ways to manage aging skin, doctors have shifted their focus to other areas, some once deemed too risky to touch. Blepharoplasty, or bleph, for short, is a plastic surgery procedure that trims, redistributes, or removes excess skin on the eyelids.

The procedure can be done to help improve peripheral vision or for more cosmetic purposes, like removing hooded eyelids. Blephs can be done on the eyelid itself, which is known as an upper bleph, or a lower bleph, which is done below the eye to treat puffy or saggy skin. Even the most effective eye creams and potent skin care formulas sometimes may not be enough to correct excess skin around the eyes, but before you schedule your consultation, see what doctors have to say about the procedure to determine if it’s the best option for you right now.

An upper bleph specifically addresses the eyelid, where skin and sometimes fat closest to the nose are removed. Dr. Lyle Leipziger, chief of plastic surgery at North Shore University Hospital and LIJ Medical Center in New York, explains that the procedure involves removing skin and excising or repositioning deeper fatty tissue to address droopiness, puffiness, and under-eye bags. “As we age, the skin around the eyes naturally loses elasticity, which can cause the upper eyelids to droop, look heavy, or create a tired appearance. This can also be exacerbated by excess upper eyelid fatty tissue,” says Leipziger. During surgery, he explains, excess skin is removed, and surgeons may also remove or reposition small amounts of fat to create a smoother, more defined upper eyelid contour. “The goal is to open up the eyes and create a brighter, more refreshed appearance while preserving the patient’s natural eye shape,” he adds.

Dr. Lesley Rabach, a board-certified facial plastic surgeon and co-founder of Rabach Aesthetics in NYC, says that the best candidates for an upper bleph are people who have a “normal” eyebrow position with excess skin, also known as dermatochalasia. “When considering an upper bleph, your surgeon needs to look at the eyebrow position, eyelid skin, and fat, as well as how much the eye opens when determining if an upper bleph is the correct procedure,” she notes. Rabach says that sometimes a brow lift and/or ptosis repair—which reconstructs the eyelid muscles or connects it to the forehead muscle to lift it—is part of the surgery as well.

Additionally, Leipziger adds that blephs are ideal for individuals who have excess skin or fullness in their upper eyelids that makes their eyes look heavy, tired, or aged. “This is especially common as we get older and lose elasticity in the delicate skin around the eyes,” he points out. “Ideal candidates are generally in good overall health, have realistic expectations, and are bothered by drooping upper eyelid skin and possible fatty tissue.” He recommends having a consultation with a board-certified plastic surgeon to determine whether overhanging skin, excess fatty tissue, brow position, or another anatomical factor is contributing to the appearance of the upper eyelids or if it’s something else.

Rabach says the results are both subtle and obvious at the same time. “They remove the extra skin that often has a crepey texture while restoring the youthful appearance of the eyelid,” she says. “An upper bleph should restore the natural youthful architecture of the upper eyelid that is natural to the person.”

By removing excess skin and, according to Leipziger, when appropriate, repositioning small amounts of fat from the upper eyelids, the procedure helps create a more open and defined eye area. “Patients should experience a significant improvement with their eyes looking brighter and less tired, without ever looking ‘done’” says Leipziger. He also notes that while an upper bleph usually lasts a long time, it doesn’t slow down or stop the way your skin will naturally age. So you may see some sagging or loss of elasticity return over time. “The goal is a natural-looking enhancement rather than a dramatic change in appearance,” Leipziger expresses.

The eye area is very delicate, but blephs are less invasive than you’d think. Rabach performs the procedure at her practice several times a week using just local anesthesia. “However, we use very thin stitches that stay in place for close to one week and must be removed. While there is little to no pain, it’s hard to camouflage the stitches, so we tell our patients they will feel perfectly fine to work from home even after the surgery that day, but not be working from the office,” says Rabach.

Leipziger agrees that recovery from a blepharoplasty is generally minimal compared to other cosmetic surgeries. He says most patients experience some swelling, bruising, and mild discomfort during the first week, which gradually improves. “I typically recommend taking about five to seven days off from work and social activities for an upper blepharoplasty, and a little longer when a lower blepharoplasty is performed,” says Leipziger, adding that most people can resume normal activities within two weeks.

For long-term eye lifting and opening, blephs are the way to go, but Leipziger says several nonsurgical treatments can help improve the appearance of the upper eyelids—even if only temporarily. It all depends on your specific concerns. “Botox can provide a subtle brow lift by relaxing specific muscles, which can help open up the eye area. Certain laser and radiofrequency treatments can also improve skin quality and mild skin laxity by stimulating collagen production and tightening the skin. These options can be beneficial for patients with early signs of aging or minimal excess skin,” says Leipziger. The biggest difference is that the nonsurgical options cannot remove excess eyelid skin or fat. “For patients with significant skin laxity or hooding, an upper blepharoplasty is typically the most effective solution for achieving a more open, refreshed appearance,” Leipziger clarifies.

According to Rabach, a fat transfer is another option to restore hollowness along the orbital bone. This technique can help smooth crepey texture that can set in with age. She also recommends lasers such as CO2 to help improve skin quality, but specifies they aren’t a replacement for surgical removal of excess skin.

As previously mentioned, eyelid skin—even if there’s less of it—will continue to age after an upper bleph. “Long-lasting results come from general health and care of the area. Always wear sunscreen and wear sunglasses to protect the skin, as it’s the thinnest skin on the body and very delicate and prone to sun damage,” says Rabach.

Along with the non-negotiable sunscreen, Leipziger recommends medical-grade skin care products and ingredients such as retinol or peptides to support skin quality. “Additionally, patients may benefit from complementary nonsurgical treatments, such as Botox, laser skin rejuvenation, and/or chemical peels. While surgery cannot stop the aging process, proper skin care and maintenance can help patients enjoy their refreshed appearance for many years,” Leipziger concludes.

#6814. Stop Hunting for Pricey Antiques—Designers Say These 9 Humble Finds Give a Home So Much Character

They’re affordable, widely available, and stylish.

It’s any avid thrifter’s dream to stumble upon an extremely valuable piece of decor while browsing the shelves of their favorite antique store. But while you’re hunting for rare treasures, don’t discount the less-expensive finds—they can be the secret to a home that looks instantly filled with character.

“To me, a collected home is much more interesting when everything isn’t perfect or chosen for its monetary value,” designer Lauren Sullivan of Well by Design tells House Beautiful. “Often, it’s the less precious pieces that give a room its personality.”

Even as older generations are continuing to downsize and offload unwanted things to local thrift stores, it still might be hard to find the diamonds in the rough. Keep reading to find out what Sullivan, and a handful of other designers, say you should be on the lookout for on your next thrifting visit.

The most valuable antiques are the ones that have been passed down through generations. In Sullivan’s own bathroom, the designer keeps her great-grandmother’s vanity set on the countertop, its aged finish providing beautiful contrast with the more contemporary marble and finishes. In her bedroom, the same great-grandmother’s small bedside clock found a place on Sullivan’s nightstand.

“Neither is particularly valuable monetarily, but I love incorporating pieces that have been passed down through generations into a home filled with both old and new,” Sullivan says.

Designers love incorporating artwork into homes to add personality and dimension, but Sullivan, as well as designer Becca Meyer of BB Meyer Design, believe that antique shops are where you can really find some hidden gems.

“Some of my favorite pieces of art have come from flea markets or antique shops for very little money,” Meyer says. “I much prefer these to mass produced prints.” You’re more likely to find pieces with aged textures at these types of shops, and that patinaed quality creates an entirely unique look.

Sullivan explains that she’s actually particularly drawn to prints, as well as smaller paintings and silhouettes. She even has an inexpensive print of The Skating Minister in a vintage frame in one of her guest rooms, pictured above. “These smaller, or sometimes quirky, pieces can bring so much personality to a space without requiring a significant investment,” she adds. They may not be originals, but they’re definitely unique.

Old pottery and ceramics are widely available, and they are usually affordable as well. Designer Brooke Snyder of Design Hutch loves how they make any place feel more curated.

The imperfections these handmade pieces carry, from uneven shapes, variations in glaze, or evidence of years of use, are what help these items bring a warmth to a room, particularly when placed near more refined materials, according to Sullivan. They feel humble, maybe even utilitarian, especially when displayed in a kitchen. Or, better yet, used in a kitchen (as long as they’re safe).

“They're beautiful on their own but also so functional for holding utensils or branches or flowers,” Meyer adds. “They make any space feel a little more lived-in and loved and help tell a story—and they don't break the bank!”

“I love incorporating vintage plates, old copper pots, and woven baskets into a home,” explains designer Joe Waroquier. Though these are run-of-the-mill, everyday objects, they can take on and provide an entirely different life when displayed in the home as decor. Mismatched plates on a wall, a collection of copper pots hanging on a rod or resting on shelves in the kitchen, an old crock filled with branches—these items invite in warmth, texture, and more personality.

“I’m particularly drawn to pieces that show their age, whether it’s a little patina, a worn finish, or an imperfect glaze,” he adds. “I love mixing these finds with more contemporary furnishings because that contrast makes a room feel collected and personal rather than overly designed.”

Mirrors can be intriguing focal points of a room, or they can add dimension to a gallery wall. However you want to style them, Snyder loves finding these reflective pieces at antique malls and vintage shops.

“Purchasing something that you don’t see every day from a retailer allows this unique piece to stand out on its own, without feeling like something you see everywhere or feels too simple,” she explains.

As Snyder puts it, antique table lamps provide high visual impact with low investment. Adding one to your accent table or on a bookshelf is an easy way to make your space feel more curated while inviting in another level of ambience with the lighting.

These vintage lamps in the above space, for instance, were bought from a collector and make the primary bedroom feel much more charming and curated. And unlike antique sconces, there’s no need to hardwire these into the wall.

Besides pottery and lamps, designer Ryann Swan Hackett of Ryann Swan Design loves to find and collect other small tabletop decor for her projects. Think candleholders, vintage coffee table books, and trays—things that can layer a little history and individuality into a room or simple vignette.

“If you’re lucky,” Swan Hackett says of the vintage coffee table books, “you’ll find an old inscription inside, which gives the book a story of its own.”

In terms of midsize accent pieces, Meyer says that she has a soft spot for finding antique stools “because you can pop them into any space to add depth and dimension.” Tuck them beside an armchair in the living room, add them to an empty kitchen corner and place a plant on top, or stick one in a bathroom by the tub for somewhere to keep a drink while relaxing. And if you find an upholstered one that could use new fabric, switching it out for a pattern you love is probably worth it.

“These bring a natural warmth that can be difficult to replicate with something new, and their small size makes them fairly reasonable in price for a vintage furnishing piece,” Meyer says.

A larger piece of furniture is likely always going to be more expensive than a smaller one, but designer Yena Jung of By Yena Designs thinks vintage hutches are the versatile antiques people should pay more attention to. They come in many different shapes, styles, and sizes, and can easily make a home in any room of the house.

“A beautiful vintage hutch can add warmth, charm, and a sense of history to a space,” Jung says. “I especially love how their imperfections and natural patina make them feel collected over time.”

#6812. Everything We Know About Coachella 2027

It’s the most wonderful time of the year: Coachella has officially dropped the list of performers for its 2027 festival, and as usual, it offers a stacked lineup across genres, with power players in pop, alternative, R&B, and more on the bill.

The headliners include Dua Lipa, SZA, and Tame Impala. Madonna will also be at the festival following the release of her most recent album, Confessions II, but will perform in the dance tent instead of a main stage—a cheeky nod to the exact same billing she received when she performed at the festival as the first dance act in 2006.

Here’s everything we know about Coachella 2027 so far, should you plan on trekking to the desert next spring.

The 2027 festival will once again take place at the Empire Polo Club in Indio, California. Coachella will run from April 9 to 11 and April 16 to 18, 2027.

In addition to Dua, Madonna, SZA, and Tame Impala, other performers include Zara Larsson, Jisoo, Kehlani, Kelela, Haute & Freddy, Phoebe Bridgers, Robyn, Adéla, Tiffany Day, Underscores, Becky G, Babymonster, Muna, Magdalena Bay, Shygirl, Victoria Monét, Caroline Polachek, and many more.

Passes for Coachella go on sale on November 13 at 12 P.M. PT. There will be different tiers—from GA to VIP and more—as well as options that include shuttle transportation to and from the festival. Passes for both weekends can be purchased here, with more information available here.

Not heading to the desert this year? No problem—YouTube plans to partner with the festival again as the exclusive livestream partner for both weekends of Coachella. Fans can watch performances on the official YouTube channel.

#6810. I Stood on a Vibration Plate Every Day for Two Weeks. I Have Thoughts.

Why is everyone suddenly raving about this vibrating plate for lymphatic drainage, muscle recovery, and more?

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

When it comes to personality types, I consider myself part of a unique subset: Type A minus. This means I am Type A most of the time, like when I’m traveling, and because my calendar and to-do list are my lifelines. Still, I lean more toward Type B when it comes to my unread text messages or how many times I snooze my alarm (hint: Both are high numbers). But one thing I am most certainly extra Type A about is my wellness routine.

I recently created a shared note on my iPhone with my friend that lists every supplement or health powder I take, when I take it, and links to the products. But my newest wellness obsession has nothing to do with an ingestible boost—it’s a device that may transport you back to an ’80s workout video à la Olivia Newton-John.

Vibration plates are all over my TikTok algorithm, but athletes widely used them in the ’70s and ’80s to improve performance and recovery. “They were first used in rehab and even in astronaut training to help prevent muscle and bone loss, but now they’ve become popular in gyms and at home for general fitness and wellness,” says Jeff Paul, the director of personal training at Chelsea Piers Fitness.

Today, it seems like everyone on my For You page has incorporated the vibrating plate into their routine, so I decided to see what all the chatter was about for myself. “Vibration plates gently vibrate while you stand, sit, or exercise on them. Those small, rapid vibrations make your muscles contract many times per second—way more than they would during a normal workout,” adds Paul. “When used correctly, vibration plates can help improve circulation and lymphatic flow, which supports recovery and reduces fluid buildup.”

It seems simple: a vibrating plate, but when you stand on the jiggling, high-motion platform (though speed settings are adjustable), your muscles are gently activated to promote lymphatic flow, aid workout recovery, and increase blood circulation. These plates claim to activate hundreds of muscles (even those your regular Pilates or cycling class can’t target), which may help slightly increase muscle mass. They also claim to increase bone density, helping to prevent osteoporosis, a condition that often occurs naturally as we age.

“Research supports vibration plates [are beneficial] for muscle strengthening, improved bone density, and reduced risk of falls," says Sarah Koehl, doctor of physical therapy at WAVE Physical Therapy & Pilates. "They can be particularly helpful for older adults or for anyone who cannot perform traditional strength training at higher loads.”

When shopping for vibration plates, I came across several categories—the two most popular being linear and oscillating, which move from side to side and tend to be gentler on joints. I opted for the latter option from LifePro. However, if you want to bulk up, Koehl adds that vibration plates provide slightly more—if not the same—muscle-mass increase as regular strength training, and a vertical plate is better for building muscle.

Vibration plates have a slew of versatile benefits. They can also be used for lymphatic drainage, which is what motivated me to adopt the device. Alternatively, you can work out on the plate, adding it to a push-up or squat routine to intensify the exercise. Using the device is pretty simple: I stand on the plate for 10 minutes a day and let the vibrations do the work. When I first started using the LifePro Vibra device, I used speed setting 2, but I’ve worked my way up to 6. What I love about the LifePro Vibra is that it is voice-controlled and comes with a remote, so I can change the speed and treatment time with my voice or a touch—without getting off the plate mid-session.

Paul urges you not to overuse a vibrating plate. “Too high a setting or too long a session can cause joint discomfort or dizziness,” he explains. “If you have a pacemaker, recent surgery, or any medical condition, definitely check with your doctor first. Otherwise, start slow and see how your body responds.”

I consistently used the LifePro Vibra for 10 minutes every morning, usually after a workout, to aid in recovery and reduce morning bloat. I’ve heard stories of people who experienced immediate bowel movements or reduced bloating after using the device. While my results haven’t been that dramatic, I’ve definitely noticed a difference since I started using it. First, my muscles feel less sore, even after trying new workouts that target different muscle groups than usual. I also love using the plate as an extra layer of lymphatic drainage because the movement kick-starts my lymphatic system and helps avoid stagnant fluid, even on days when I don’t work out.

"Vibration plates are not a magic solution," Paul says. "You’ll get the most out of them when you pair the vibration with movement. So instead of just standing on it, try squats, lunges, or balance work. That’s when you’ll see real improvements in muscle engagement, circulation, and stability. On their own, they’re more of a supplement to your training than a replacement for it."

One thing to note: the device is bulky, especially in a small New York City apartment like mine. However, it can easily slide under a bed or behind a dresser. Whether you want to channel an ’80s fitness routine or improve lymphatic drainage, vibration plates are definitely worth trying if you have the space.

Tory Burch, Balenciaga, and Chloé are already up to 40 percent off.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

You don’t have to hold out for Black Friday to shop for a new handbag—fashion sales have already begun. Retailers like Amazon, Nordstrom, and Revolve marked down dozens of styles from popular labels like Tory Burch and Mansur Gavriel, to name a few. And with discounts up to 60 percent, many of these designer finds will sell out before Thanksgiving dinner even hits the table, so we don’t recommend waiting until the holiday weekend to shop.

To help you beat the rush, we’ve shortlisted the six best sites for early Black Friday handbag deals. Whether you’re looking to upgrade your everyday work tote, pick up a new party bag before the holidays, or check a few stylish people off your gifting list early, these online destinations are worth your time.

If you’re planning to shop Amazon’s massive Black Friday and Cyber Monday sale, it’s worth breaking your trip up into a few smaller, more manageable purchases. In years past, we’ve seen early fashion deals sell out (or, arguably worse, return to full price) before the event officially begins. Everyday bags from entry-level designers like Marc Jacobs, Kate Spade, and Michael Kors are already deeply discounted. #6805. Every Outfit TV Is the Digital-Age Fashion Police We Never Knew We Needed

The founders of @everyoutfitonSATC spill the tea on their latest venture into video podcasting

“There are some things that need to be seen to be believed,” says Lauren Garroni, the brunette half of @EveryOutfitonSATC—a Sex and the City fashion archive Instagram account turned Fashion Police-style podcast that she runs with her blonde counterpart, Chelsea Fairless. She’s talking about a stretched canvas image of Dave Navarro walking in the Anna Sui Spring-Summer 1997 runway show, which is currently being sold online by a well-known publishing company. But she might as well have been speaking about the pair’s latest venture: Every Outfit TV.

“Every Outfit is a weekly rundown of the comings and goings in pop culture and fashion,” explains Garroni. She and Fairless launched their podcast in 2021, around the time that the SATC spinoff And Just Like That was announced. Devotees will recall that it was actually phase two, phase one being an Instagram account born in 2016 from Fairless’s frustration with the lack of variety when it came to photos of SATC outfits on the internet. Ten years later, the podcast is viral, the Instagram has 709k followers, and the overall project has morphed into Every Outfit TV, a video component available exclusively on Patreon.

The video format offers Fairless and Garroni the chance to do things that wouldn’t work with solely audio. (Note: not every new podcast episode will have video.) “We grew up being obsessed with the VH1 Vogue Fashion Awards and stuff like House of Style,” says Fairless. The brilliantly lo-fi, green-screen-style opening sequence posted to their Instagram account last week distills the energy they’re going for with its nods to The Nanny, Clueless, and Access Hollywood.

Podcast listeners, TV viewers, and Instagram followers can expect new franchises like “It’s Always Fashion Week Somewhere” and “The Runway Rewind,” which will showcase the pair’s thoughts on runway shows while tapping into the tabloid entertainment vibe that they both admire. There will also be Oprah Winfrey-style book club episodes and “Top 10” lists fiercely curated by Garroni and Fairless themselves.

Ask the duo why now was the ideal time to pivot, and Fairless answers bluntly. “Lauren forced me to,” she says. Garroni sees it differently. “Chelsea finally succumbed to being on camera,” she refutes. Really, the two are responding to a general podcast-world shift to video, a medium that enables them to “fulfill an itch,” says Fairless. She didn’t have cable in her house as a child, prompting her to develop an obsession with fashion magazines and websites like First View (RIP). Garroni’s upbringing makes up for it. “My parents just gave me unrestricted access to television, cable, and the internet from a young age,” she says. Now, both of them are self-diagnosed as chronically online and cite hours on TikTok and Instagram as the reason why they collectively possess the knowledge of someone who has a PhD in fashion history.

While this wealth of niche information could potentially alienate an audience, Garroni and Fairless’s playful banter (think Siskel and Ebert, or Waldorf and Statler) makes up for it. They don’t shy away from cross-generational references, either. “We have laundered our older gay men references that we learned from watching E! News and E! True Hollywood Story,” says Garroni.

In the end, the duo intends to be authentically themselves. “I think Lauren and I are examples of millennials who are very culturally Gen X,” says Fairless. “We were always more interested in Gen X things, Gen X people. I didn’t give a shit about the Backstreet Boys when I was a teenager. We were into Hole and Pavement.”

It would be remiss not to talk about the hit television series that started it all. At this point, they have documented every outfit on Sex and the City more than once in the past ten years, but developments in the show’s universe kept them going. “Something would always happen to prevent us from abandoning this,” says Fairless. Garroni agrees, recalling when Cynthia Nixon ran for governor of New York in 2018. (They released a satirical book We Should All Be Mirandas a year later. Garroni claims to be a Miranda with a Carrie rising, while Fairless is a Miranda with a Samantha rising.) “It always sustained us,” says Garroni. “Every moment we thought, ‘Okay, how long can this joke go on for?’”

If Fairless has anything to do with it, the answer to that question is forever. “Sex and the City is never done. If there is one thing I know to be true in this life, it will never go away,” she says. “It will always change forms and there will always be something newsworthy to talk about.”

Asked what else is coming down the pipeline, Garroni is quick to mention field pieces that will take them out of their impeccably designed Los Angeles set, complete with vintage gray furniture designed in the ’90s by architect David Chipperfield and a pink carpet. (It was modeled after a mashup of Halston’s New York City apartment—an Every Outfit hyperfixation—and the Chicago department store Carson Pirie Scott.) Fairless, however, has other goals in mind. She’d like to add more single-breasted blazers to her wardrobe rotation, she says. Also: “I want hair and makeup.”

#6804. These Tinted SPFs Protect Your Under-Eyes and Conceal Dark Circles

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Sunscreen is always important, but when one product can also brighten your complexion and take the place of your favorite concealer, it becomes impossible to forget. Tinted SPF offers the best of both worlds, helping protect your skin from the sun while giving it the appearance of a more even complexion. And when it’s made specifically for the eye area, it can make one of the most delicate parts of your skin care routine feel a whole lot easier.

“Tinted sunscreens are one of those rare beauty products where the cosmetic benefit and the dermatological benefit really align,” says Dr. Pearl Ugwu-Dike, a board-certified dermatologist. “You’re protecting your skin from tomorrow’s sun damage while making it look good today.” As for why they’re especially useful under the eyes, the skin in that area is some of the most delicate that we have on our body. “That makes it particularly susceptible to visible signs of photoaging, like fine lines, crepiness, hyperpigmentation,” Dr. Ugwu-Dike says. “It’s also often a place that gets neglected when applying sunscreen, conventionally.” That’s why the best tinted SPFs for under-eyes are so special: They offer protection while providing immediate color correction and a brighter-looking appearance, helping to camouflage under-eye bags and dark circles.

Of course, the right formula depends on how much coverage you want, your skin type, and how your under-eye area tends to behave throughout the day. Some options are sheer and brightening, while others can pull double duty as a true concealer. Ahead, we’ve rounded up the best tinted SPFs for under-eyes so you can find the one that fits seamlessly into your morning routine.

This is what happens when an eye cream, sunscreen, and concealer all decide to overachieve. “I love that it addresses two of my biggest concerns with the under-eye area, which are pigmentation and photoaging,” says Dr. Pearl Ugwu-Dike, an NYC-based board-certified dermatologist. “I also really like that it combines peptides, hyaluronic acid, and a cooling applicator.”

#6803. This Colorful South Carolina Home Marries Mediterranean Elegance With Lowcountry Charm

Designer Megan Molten worked handmade details and custom elements into every room.

How do you blend the magical ease of Mediterranean life with the coastal charm of South Carolina’s lowcountry? After an enchanting vacation in Greece with their three daughters, Megan Molten’s clients wanted their new build on Daniel Island to capture that unique, transportive feeling they fell in love with—and they asked the Charleston-based designer to help them achieve it.

Alongside builder Wes Whittle of EasTech Property Development, Molten was involved from the very beginning, crafting the blank canvas into a residence that balanced the sun-drenched elegance of the Greek islands with traditional Lowcountry living. The designer went all in on every detail, relying on high-quality craftsmanship, custom pieces, and handmade elements to bring life to every room.

Upon entry, guests are greeted by a hand-painted, botanical-themed mural by local artist Jenn Griffith. “The mural’s objective was very specific,” says Molten. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry.” Turn to the other side and find a plaster-covered stairwell by artist Emily Pope Harris, an addition that sets the tone for the whole home. Move further into the home and find a hand-painted floor that shines in the craft room, created using a stencil from Etsy and a few Sherwin-Williams paint colors—it’s playfully fabulous and still somehow timeless. “Clients do not typically splurge in rooms that are not front and center, so this was a real treat to bring to life, especially knowing how much their daughters love to craft,” the designer adds.

Elsewhere, a larger portion of the budget was split between the lighting and the kitchen. “Statement lighting in the house was always prioritized,” Molten says. “The homeowners also agreed that the lighting was important and needed to be special. I was finally able to use some light fixtures that I have been eyeing for years!”

As for the kitchen, it was more difficult to know where to splurge and where to save, a predicament the designer finds herself with on most projects. However, looking now at the honed limestone backsplash, green quartzite countertops with an integrated sink, and a plaster hood by Pope Harris, it’s clear that she made the right choices.

Even with all the home’s beautiful elements, the fan-favorite among Molten’s team ended up being the pool just outside the kitchen. It’s lower than most Molten has worked on in Charleston, making it one of the most unique she’s seen. Flanked by archways and paired with two spas and a raised hot tub, it fully captures a Grecian grotto feel.

Even with all the one-of-a-kind features and elevated furnishings, the home is a place where life is meant to be experienced to the fullest. “The spaces are to be enjoyed without hesitation,” Molten explains, “creating a beautiful, practical environment for a life well lived.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Megan MoltenArchitect: DLB Custom Home DesignBuilder: EasTech Property DevelopmentLocation: Daniel Island, South CarolinaThe Space: A six-bedroom, seven-bathroom new build spanning 6,000 square feet.

The beauty of this entryway starts outside with the front door. Made locally by Maebeck Doors, it’s an arched shape with a grid pattern that serves as a charming “wow” moment upon entering the home.

Once inside the house, the awe continues, from the curved plaster stairwell to the custom botanical mural by artist Jenn Griffith. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry,” Molten adds. The final touch? A stone table in the notch at the base of the stairs, perfect for placing keys or bags at the end of the day.

Molten’s client wanted a large, comfortable sectional that could easily seat her family and any guests, so when the team saw these two at High Point Market, they knew they were perfect for the space. A travertine table, nestled between the two sectionals, made the pieces feel like one long, continuous design, and both were upholstered in Crypton performance fabric for added durability.

To complement the space, Molten added a plush armchair and topped the room off with a modern light fixture that doesn’t add much noise. “The artwork above the sectional is from the homeowner’s own collection,” Molten says. “It served as the catalyst for the colorways used throughout the home.”

A calming, functional space, the brightness of the kitchen is achieved not through a classic all-white kitchen, but through shades of light green, from the quartzite countertops to the greenish-gray cabinets. Rattan pendant lights bring in that casual Mediterranean feel, especially since the floor-to-ceiling windows on the left overlook the pool below.

To keep it family-friendly, Molten applied one of her favorite tricks: leather on the counter stool seats and fabric on the back. “Wiping off messes and letting the leather patina is both practical and pretty,” she adds.

This dining nook really needed to be functional, since it’s where Molten’s clients and their three children sit for breakfast every morning and dinner every night. Durability for daily use played a huge part in the furniture choices; they sourced a long wooden table and used the same seating upholstery trick here as they did with the counter stools in the kitchen. For a touch of whimsy, Molten crowned the nook with a coco bead chandelier.

Instead of plaster on the double-sided fireplace as she had initially intended, Molten went with a zellige tile façade that produces a lovely shine.

Easily accessible from the living room, kitchen, and screened-in porch, this breakfast area—complete with a full bar—is perfect for hosting. Anyone can come in and make themselves a drink whenever they want.

Arched upper cabinets with lattice details create more movement in this space, as well as add in charm, and the chandelier has been on the designer’s list of favorites for a while. “I particularly love the contrast of the black countertop with the coral cabinets,” Molten says.

The first thing Molten selected for the scullery was the charming lattice window over the sink looking out onto the yard. Then came the bold wallpaper, which inspired the vibrant cabinet paint color, balanced out with a light Calacatta marble countertop with hints of yellow and gold throughout.

“This scullery is a very functional space that houses their microwave, coffee maker, refrigerator, and drawers with snacks, all built-in and panel-ready,” Molten says.

When her clients told her they wanted a dedicated craft room for their girls, Molten was just as excited at the prospect as their daughters. “A dedicated specialty room like this typically provides a great opportunity for lots of creativity and color,” she explains.

They started with the colorful striped wallpaper on the ceiling, pulling colors from it for the built-in cubby shelf and the painted floors. A neutral light fixture keeps the room from feeling too busy, giving it a more timeless feel. “The floor design is so special, and a bit of a splurge,” Molten adds. “I was so pleased the client embraced this idea!”

The unique wallpaper served as the starting point for this powder bath, as Molten designed everything else around it. “The Jamie Young mirror looks like an oyster shell, and the floating stone was a nice feature,” she says. “Painting the trim and ceiling the pink shell color made the wallpaper, tile, and stone come together.”

Molten had never done a wallpaper border before, but she adores how it turned out in one of the daughter’s bedrooms. “It’s a great way to add thoughtful design details to a room and is cost-effective too, as opposed to covering the full room in wallpaper,” she says. The trim color was pulled from this border, which she paired with a signature shade of pink from her own collection with Sherwin-Williams.

“For the daughter’s bathroom, we opted for a tub/shower combo to accommodate young children,” Molten explains. These children-friendly details are found elsewhere in the room too, like a pull-out step stool under the sink cabinets.

The pink-and-green palette from the daughter’s bedroom is continued here, though in slightly more saturated shades. A local Charleston brand, Mirth Studio, created the painted wood valance to pair with the Pepper Home shower curtain, and Pope Harris continued her plaster work here, covering a Visual Comfort & Co. sconce over the mirror in a custom pink hue.

Located on the small third floor, the only rooms that occupy this level are the office, the office den, and a guest suite, making it a quiet place to escape when the clients, who share this space, need to get work done. “Our client loved the Tiger Palm wallpaper, so we applied that on all of the walls and at the low part of the angled ceiling to help mask all of the ceiling lines in this room,” Molten says. They even have their own wet bar up here for post-work happy hour.

Designed as a dedicated no-kids zone off the office, this private room is where Molten’s clients get away to watch movies after bedtime, decompress after a long day, and just retreat to when they need time to themselves.

“We opted for a performance velvet on the sofa with velvet textured pillows to create a luxurious, cozy feel,” the designer says. “I love the Currey & Co. chandelier with green glass—it works so perfectly in this small room.”

“Like the primary bedroom and primary bathroom, the closet is not huge, but it’s efficient and thoughtfully designed,” Molten explains. With her team, they designed a wall of built-ins and paid close attention to the functionality of each drawer and shelf while maximizing the space; then a local cabinet maker brought them to life.

Molten chose soft, luxurious carpet that feels like stepping into a high-end department store and a fabulous Made Goods light fixture.

The architect who worked on this house, Damien Busillo of DLB Custom Home Design, had this lower-level pool incorporated in the original plans, and Molten was able to assist with the tile selections.

“This is such a creative way to use empty space under the house,” she says. “Homes in Charleston are often raised when built due to coastal flooding; however, this was the most interesting and exciting design concept I have seen for this area of the home.” It truly captures that Grecian grotto vibe.

Megan Molten has always been creative, but her love of interiors didn’t fully manifest until she bought and decorated her first house in Charleston, South Carolina. From there, she started experimenting with design as a hobby, sharing her eye with friends and family, before devoting all her time to it and finally deciding to open her own eponymous firm. Now, she shares her elevated coastal style and luxurious Lowcountry vibe with clients across the country.

#6802. You Can Definitely Do These Easy Nail Art Designs at Home

Take it from a fellow amateur manicurist.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Ah, the at-home manicure. Nail artists make it look so easy when you’re in the salon, but get in front of a file and dotting tool at home? Suddenly, things can get a little shaky. I’ve technically been doing my own nails since I was about six, but really started leaning into trying to get better at painting on little designs over the past few years. I can say from experience: There is a lot of so-called easy nail art out there that, in reality, is very difficult to do yourself. But there are also plenty of designs you can totally pull off as a beginner. That, my friends, is what we’re here for today.

Unfortunately, the old “practice makes perfect” thing does actually apply to any type of creative endeavor, manicures included. I’m not saying you’re going to be able to perfectly recreate any of these looks the first time you try (though, if you can, maybe quit your day job). There’s a reason I still pay a professional to do my nails if I want them to look extra super good for a special occasion; those people have years of training I’ll never be able to catch up to. Instead, these are designs with elements I know a beginner can do well enough that you won’t get so frustrated with the results that you give up on your new hobby for good.

“It can be super intimidating to start painting your nails while you're looking at an inspo pic or video from IG or Pinterest, but remember that the reason you probably started painting your nails was to have a creative outlet and a moment of self expression,” nail artist and wise person Sigourney Nuñez says. “Try to not let perfectionism get in the way of something that is meant to be a self care moment where you get to show up for yourself in this fun, creative and colorful way.”

Hear, hear! If you’re ready to get started on your artistic ungual journey, keep scrolling for expert-recommended tips, tools, and plenty of design inspiration.

While your toolkit can grow exponentially the further you get into your nail art journey, Nuñez says the “perfect DIY tool trifecta” is a clean up brush, dotting tool, and liner brush.

I can confirm the clean up brush will be your most-used tool when you’re learning to do your own nails. Even if you don’t make a massive, colored-outside-the-lines mess, going around your nail with a clean up brush dipped in nail polish remover “can really give you that crispy salon finish at home,” Nuñez says. She uses a Simply Simmons #4 painter’s brush you can buy at Michael’s, but says you could also sacrifice an old angled eyeliner brush if you aren’t looking to spend money on tools yet. “Just be sure you don’t use it on your eyes again after you’ve dunked it in nail polish remover,” she warns.

Nuñez calls dotting tools “the gateway to the nail art universe.” You could use bobby pins, a flat eraser on the end of a pencil, or the end of a brush, but there are plenty of affordable dotting tool options too. Olive & June makes a nice double-sided one that would be a great first dotting tool, while Halo Taco has a fun, holographic set that comes with a total of eight different sized tips.

Once you’re really ready to level up, get yourself a nail brush. There are sets with multiple brush sizes out there, but Nuñez doesn’t think you need them. “I literally only use one type of liner brush, [the] Liner 3 from Bio Seaweed Gel,” she says. “It’s been my go-to brush for years.” Instead of dealing with a bunch of different size brushes, you can make different size lines by varying the amount of pressure you use.

I cannot emphasize this enough: If you’re serious about wanting your manicure to look as close to professionally done as possible, you absolutely must prep your nails before you even think about picking up your base coat. This means completely removing all your polish, cutting and/or shaping your nails with a file, and cleaning up your cuticle area... but not too much on that last part.

“When it comes to DIY prep, less is more,” Nuñez says. “There is no need to over trim any surrounding skin unless there's a hangnail that's waving hi at you.” Instead, she recommends using a cuticle buffer stick like the Sally Hansen Push & Buff 2-in-1 Cuticle Pen. “It helps exfoliate the cuticle area and side walls, plus helps buff out the waxy layer that can accumulate [on your nails],” Nuñez says. “That waxy layer is totally normal but it gets in the way of adhesion.” I like to wash my hands after I’ve done all that trimming, filing, and buffing, to make sure there’s no leftover nail dust that could ruin my hard work.

Keeping your cuticles healthy between manis will make this prep step easier. “I’m actually so passionate about cuticle care,” Brooklyn-based nail artist Anna Miles says. “A little attention every day will do wonders—more than any nail tech could accomplish in our monthly sessions.” She recommends using a towel to gentle scrub your cuticle area after a shower (”to encourage the cuticle skin to roll back”) and be diligent about using hand cream and cuticle oil after you wash your hands.

“If you’re like me and out of sight equals out of mind, buy travel sizes in multiples and keep one everywhere—bag, coffee table, bathroom, bedside,” Miles says. She loves the CND Rescue Rxx Daily Keratin Treatment and Zoya Healing Dry Skin Hand & Body Cream, both of which come in smaller sizes.

The number one thing that will make your nail art look better is to learn to leverage your fulcrum finger (aka the digit that’s holding your hand steady while you work; Nuñez says this is usually the pinky) and elbows. Do not try to do your nails while sitting on a couch; you need a table with enough space to create those stabilizing points.

Whether you’re going to use a dotting tool, liner brush, sponge, or other nail art tool, it can take some practice to figure out how much polish to put on it before you start designing. I always do a test run on a piece of paper; it’s not a perfect substitution for painting on your curved nail, but I think it gives you a pretty good idea of how much polish to use. Nuñez also has a handy video on how to load your liner brush properly.

And, again, you just need to practice. When I’m in the habit of doing my nails every week, I’m shocked at how much better I get over the course of a month. Ready to dive in? Here’s some inspo to get you started.

Let’s start with the easiest nail art of them all, a skittles manicure. This is simply painting each nail with a different shade of polish, which is a fun way to start playing around with color theory—a key skill for professional-looking DIY manis. “Every great manicure starts with a beautiful color story,” Nuñez says. “When the colors can do most of the talking, you don't have to try as hard with your DIY design.”

Glitter polish is kind of a nail art hack in its own right, because the texture makes any mistakes kinda blend in. For this look, I swiped some random splotches of purple cream over a glitter from Mooncat (a brand that makes some of the best sparkles, IMO).

The half and half manicure is a great way to practice creating straight lines: Since you fill in the space on one side of the line, it’s less obvious if things are little crooked.

“When painting lines, instead of moving the brush, you can try rotating your hand/nail while keeping the brush anchored,” Nuñez advises. “This can help with a more precise application.”

I know, I know, all these straight lines look intimidating, but, similar to the half and half, I promise that the fact that you fill in the triangles after you paint on the lines makes it far easier to get a crisp look. And if you have the patience, you could always wait for the base color to try, then use Scotch tape to stencil out the details.

Marble looks complicated, but Miles swears it’s not so difficult. “Marble is good for beginners because the success of this design is in the imperfection,” she is. “Uneven, shaky lines and blurry, smeared areas... sounds like the makings of a gorgeous marble!” Pick two colors of polish with high contrast to make the marble design stand out.

Miles says there are many ways to do marble, but one of the first methods she fell in love with is using an unused kitchen sponge that’s been cut into thin slices. Start by applying one of your polishes as a base coat, then paint the other color onto a nail-sized section of your sponge slice. “You can see the marble ‘veins’ on the sponge as you paint it,” Miles says. Stamp the wet paint onto your nail, using a rocking motion so it covers the whole nail. You can add metallic accents or a shimmery top coat to really make it special.

This is one of my go-to designs. You paint on two coats on your base color, then go in with a second color but stop before you get too close to your cuticle. It’s extremely simple, but gets loads of compliments.

If you crave more intricate designs but don’t have the brush skillset just yet, stickers are a great option. “I love using nail art stickers,” Miles says, noting that her favorites come from Deco Beauty. “For best results, make sure to apply them to a long-dried base color and paint two thin top coats instead of one thick one if you’re using traditional polish.”

Polka dot nails stay trendy, so that dotting tool is definitely worth the investment. There are many different ways to do dots—a bunch of super tiny dots, big circles, a mix and match of multiple size. I’d recommend practicing on a piece of paper first so you can get the pressure just right for the size you’re looking for. A light touch is key once you’re actually on the nail. “If your base color is not fully dry and you put too much force on the dotting tool that can create a dent and hole in your hard work,” Nuñez says.

This is a design that definitely requires Nuñez’s recommended stabilization techniques, but if things get a little shaky, you can just say you were going for a Bambi effect. Larger dots can also be a bit more forgiving.

Another trick for adding details when you don’t have a super steady hand is to apply the color you want for the detail as the base coat and then layer thick strokes of another color on top. That’s what I did for this look: The blue polish went down first, then I just painted one strip of green vertically and one strip horizontally to create the rectangle accent effect.

As everything gets digitized, I fear we’ll lose important ancient techniques, like using paper hole protectors as a stencil for a negative space half moon effect. At least, that’s how I’d pull off this look.

In the vein of nail stickers, confetti polish is a great way to create nail art without even looking at an extra tool. (KB Shimmer makes really fun ones that come in a bunch of shapes and colors.) Just make sure to apply two thin coats of top coat so the sparkles stay put.

I would never in a million years suggest that a French manicure is easy. But I will tell you that those rounded silicone stamper tools absolutely work to create a micro French, especially on shorter nails.

Using a rhinestone application tool can be a bit tricky to master, but I do find it a relatively easy way to add just a touch of detail to your nails. These won’t stick with just some good top coat; you’ll need to use nail glue too.

Great news: Once you’ve gotten good at polka dots, you can totally do flowers too. These are made up of just five black dots and one white one, but the final look seems much more complicated.

Kara McGrath is a contributing beauty editor at Cosmo with over 15 years of experience writing professionally about nails. She’s been an amateur nail artist for over 30.
