#6801. Faux Fur, Tuxedo Dressing, and Other Coat Trends Taking Over This Winter

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

These are the styles to know as the weather gets colder

A great winter outfit deserves an equally great coat, especially when it’s an essential layer for the next several months. Beyond the practical need for warmth, outerwear often sets the tone for an entire look as the first layer you see. A sharply tailored wool coat can make jeans and a sweater feel more polished, while an oversize silhouette or rich texture brings dimension and new proportions to otherwise clean-lined looks.

For fall/winter 2026, designers are rethinking classic outerwear through distinctive silhouettes, textures, and details. At Ferragamo, Tory Burch, and Fendi, precise tailoring took a dressier turn, with slim silhouettes, tuxedo-inspired designs, and satin lapels bringing eveningwear polish to wool coats. Meanwhile, Ralph Lauren, Mugler, and Burberry embraced leather for a sleeker city-ready take on winter dressing. Faux fur took on a distinctly vintage feel at Stella McCartney, Bottega Veneta, and Khaite, while plush collars added texture to everything from elegant overcoats to more everyday designs.

Whether you’re looking to invest in a forever piece or simply want something fresher than the coat you’ve worn for the past few winters, these six trends offer plenty of inspiration.

Faux fur is taking a distinctly retro turn this winter, with plush textures and elegant silhouettes reminiscent of vintage finds. Stella McCartney’s floor-grazing belted coat and Bottega Veneta’s voluminous fur both recall the glamour of the ’30s and ’40s, while Khaite’s shaggy cream jacket has more of a ’70s feel, particularly when dressed down with jeans. Of course, we love a faux-fur coat as the finishing touch to a dressy outfit, but there’s something even chicer about pairing one with everyday denim and boots.

Arma Verena Reversible Shearling and Leather Coat

For fall/winter 2026, the black evening coat is having a moment. Designers showcased styles with luxe updates to keep things more interesting. Think sharp, menswear-inspired tailoring with a more formal finish, as seen at Celine, to elegant satin coats at Dries Van Noten. It’s the go-to coat to wear to upcoming holiday parties and formal dinners, or if you just want to upgrade your traditional winter coat for something a bit more luxurious this season.

Polo Ralph Lauren Wool and Silk Crepe Tuxedo Duster Coat

The Row Erdene Single-Breasted Wool Long Coat

There’s a certain refinement to a beautifully tailored coat, especially when the details borrow from formalwear. Think precise cuts, elongated silhouettes, and tuxedo-inspired touches that feel polished enough for an evening out. There’s a more dressed-up sensibility to these classic designs—as seen at Ferragamo, Tory Burch, and Fendi—from the precise fit to the smallest finishing details. This attention to detail brings a beautiful sense of sophistication to workwear and when paired with simple everyday separates.

Gap x Victoria Beckham Wool-Blend Chesterfield Coat

Mackage Elodie-R Double-Breasted Wool-Blend Coat

When a puffer or classic wool coat feels too predictable, a leather coat offers a much bolder alternative. Ralph Lauren, Mugler, and Burberry all embraced waist-defining silhouettes, from classic double-breasted trenches to softer robe-like shapes. In dramatic maxi lengths, the effect is especially sleek, with just the right amount of edge, bringing a bit of bite to winter dressing.

Nili Lotan Arsene Leather Double-Breasted Trench Coat

The Frankie Shop Edra Shearling-Trimmed Wrap Coat

Gray was ubiquitous on runways from New York to Paris, appearing in everything from textured tweed to draped wool and sharply tailored shapes. Whether you prefer deep charcoal or soft dove, it’s an easy neutral to wear in tonal layers or to balance bright colors and prints.

Banana Republic Herringbone Wool-Blend Top Coat

Marant Etoile Pilda Herringbone Double-Breasted Coat

’S Max Mara Scarf-Detail Virgin-Wool Wrap Coat

Faux fur was all over the fall/winter 2026 runways, even in smaller doses like plush collars. If you know that feeling of a sharp chill hitting your neck when the rest of you is bundled up, these collars offer the warmth you need in an ultra-elegant way. On everything from sporty puffers and classic toggle-front wool coats, as seen at Louis Vuitton, to the patterned and pretty pastel trims at Chanel and Gabriela Hearst, it’s a detail that’s as practical as it is beautiful.

Max Mara Medusa Belted Puffer Coat with Faux-Fur Trim

#6797. How Olivia Munn Takes the Edge Off

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The actress shares the beauty and self-care essentials she swears by, including a $75 volumizing cream

Welcome to Take the Edge Off, a series that breaks down the beauty and self-care routines of influencers, CEOs, experts, and celebrities. Find out how they unwind and decompress, and take a closer look at the holy-grail essentials they’re raving about.

Life can throw unexpected surprises—good and bad—your way, and many lean on wellness practices to stay grounded in chaos. Olivia Munn knows this firsthand.

The actress was diagnosed with early-stage Luminal B breast cancer in early 2023, thanks to what she refers to as the “life-saving” risk assessment test with renowned obstetric and gynecologic surgeon Thaïs Aliabadi at the helm. With a toddler and fresh off a double mastectomy, Munn turned to meditation to find her footing through it all.

“I always associated meditation with having to quiet your mind, and I couldn’t imagine how anyone actually did that, but over time, I’ve come to understand that it isn’t about stopping the noise in your head; it’s about learning not to be carried away by it,” Munn tells Bazaar. “I don’t feel the need to chase every thought or immediately make sense of everything. I can just let things settle without feeling responsible for figuring them all out.”

That same strength and balance are exactly what fashion label Anne Klein wanted to embody when the brand enlisted Munn as ambassador for its fall/winter 2026 collection and to continue spreading her message.

Ahead, we caught up with Munn about what else keeps her present during times of stress, from the manifestation candle she loves to light to words she lives by.

“The future you is depending on the you today to do the right thing.” This means the me today depended on the me three years ago to go to the doctor, get the MRI, and face cancer head-on. I was depending on the me last week, month, or year to start working out more, learn that new language, or organize my closet. When I think about how fast time goes by, and how tomorrow, next month, and next year come in the blink of an eye, it motivates me to take the first step toward whatever I want to do.

A couple of months ago, I began using prescription-strength azelaic acid and tretinoin. I start with azelaic acid, then tretinoin, and finish with the Volume Cream by Adipeau, my holy-grail moisturizer.

My favorite cream right now is Adipeau’s Volume Cream. I’ve reached the point in my life where my hands and neck are showing signs of being crepey. A friend told me about this cream and promised me that if I used it every day for a month, I’d see a difference, and she was right. The science behind it is incredible, so incredible that I often worry about whether the company is doing well because I need them to stay in business forever.

My makeup goal has always been no-makeup makeup. But it is incredibly difficult to master, especially on yourself. My hat’s off to all the influencers who do it so beautifully; I see you and wish I were you.

Shige brand cuticle oil. The amazing manicurist Shige [Taylor] created her own nail line, beginning with an incredible cuticle oil made in Japan. [My] nail bed has never looked or felt so hydrated.

Yes, I light candles from Aloha Elixir. They’re made in Hawaii and are really special, intentional candles.

When I start getting anxiety, which I often do, or have difficulty falling asleep, I do this breathing technique that activates the parasympathetic nervous system, which controls our “fight-or-flight” response: Breathe in through your nose for four beats, hold for seven, breathe out through your nose for eight beats. It calms my system down, and the anxiety goes away, or I drift off to sleep. I do it three to six times, sometimes a little more. But it always works for me.

#6796. Mark Ballas Responds to Speculation He Might Become a Judge on ‘Dancing With the Stars’

#6795. I'm the Friend Everyone Texts for Tableware Recs. Here's Where I Send Them

Consider this your shortcut to a beautifully dressed table.

The art of tablescaping starts with some really beautiful tableware. No disrespect to placemats, but the glasses, plates, and cutlery that your guests use tend to take the spotlight. Everything tastes better coming out of a fun wine glass. And presentation is key. The right serving platter makes your weeknight leftovers look Michelin-starred. We know your design taste is as unique as your palate, so we wanted to make your shopping experience as seamless as possible. Below, we reveal over a dozen of the very best tableware brands on the market.

Whether you’re a frequent dinner party host or you’re romanticizing dinners for one, you deserve to find something truly unique for your dining table. We focused our search on dinnerware, glassware, cutlery, and serving essentials. (Table linens matter, but that’s a whole other article.) Of course, we started with the pieces filling our editors’ kitchens and dining rooms. That’s where we keep the cake plates for our favorite little treats, and high-quality wine glasses that aren’t too fragile for busy kitchens. But trust us, after going to the best design markets in the country and abroad, these are the brands that wow us year after year. They’re built to last and make every host’s life way easier.

East Fork was founded by Henri Matisse’s great-grandson, and the artistry shows in every piece. The hand-thrown pottery is crafted from regionally sourced clay in Asheville, North Carolina. The lightly speckled, colorful stoneware plates are a favorite of Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie, especially the Matisse Cake Plate sets.

Fable is a tableware brand designed for the frequent family dinner host. The brand’s Canyon Clay collection brings a warm, sunbaked terracotta to their durable serveware. It’s dishwasher-safe and meant to hold up to your messiest meals, with a touch of elegance.

If you’re looking for the kind of beautiful plates and cups that can hold up to outdoor use, look no further than Thomas Fuchs’s melamine collection. These chip-proof, shatter-proof plates are designed for al fresco meals, and the half-and-half design adds some playful color.

We already loved Hommey for their viral striped cotton bedding, but the brand has brought that signature look to your tabletop. Even your cutlery deserves a little color-drenching, and certain colors are already selling out. It’s the kind of small upgrade that makes every meal feel a bit more special.

Let’s talk glassware: no one does clarity like Simon Pearce. It catches the light beautifully. Every single glassware piece from this brand is handmade by one of a few expert artisans, and the imperfection of handmade goods is celebrated here. (You can even get the extra quirky ones—called Seconds—for a discounted price.)

Chances are you’ve seen Serax tableware in a designer department store. The brand has decades of tabletop excellence under its belt, and its designer collaborations result in delightfully creative pieces. The Carte Blanche collection, designed to look like hand-drawn sketches, is one of our favorites.

Anthropologie is the home of little luxuries for everywhere in your home, but we’re especially partial to their small space-friendly tabletop pieces. Espresso cups and dessert plates just make breakfast feel way more glamorous. The gilded Bistro Tile collection is perfect for anyone wanting some Parisian flair in their kitchen.

Sabre is another iconic name in the world of tabletop, and one look at their stainless steel cutlery sets will tell you why. You can choose handles crafted from teak wood, tortoiseshell or a glossy lacquer, and you’ll love setting the table with them at dinnertime.

It really is the little things that make a difference, and at Mogutable, those are the irresistibly cute chopsticks rests. Imagine a few of these adorable food-shaped rests scattered across your dinner table, or letting each dinner guest select one of their own before noodles are served.

Arhaus is already one of our favorite furniture brands, so it’s no surprise the sophisticated home destination has incredible serveware. Their dinnerware sets get pretty pricey, so we suggest starting with their hosting essentials: napkin rings, coasters, and cocktail shakers that help you host like a pro.

Would you believe it if we told you those are paper plates? Table Two Studio is all about disposable plates for easy cleanup, without sacrificing style. The botanical patterns remind us of archival fabric prints from our favorite textile houses. Your guests will feel extra special, and you won’t spend hours doing dishes.

The Museum of Modern Art really knows how to curate an inspiring collection, and that extends to their home goods. It’s McKenzie’s first stop whenever she wants to find a cool hostess gift. Their glassware is unmatched, especially for any fans of colored glass or playful motifs.

Fishs Eddy dinnerware pays homage to a symbol of Americana and hospitality: the diner. Their green-band dishes, checkered plates, and simple white mugs are all evocative of a steaming diner breakfast after a long night. All of that cozy nostalgia warms up your family meals.

If you love all things spiraled and swirly, this is the glassware brand for you. The delicate pops of color and squiggled stems on the glass pitchers make even water look like a fun beverage. Perfect for cocktail hour, and sure to be treasured.

If you want to spot an Ichendorf Milano glass in the wild, look for colorful motifs from nature. A stem shaped like Lily of the Valley flowers or a fish at the bottom of a tumbler are telltale signs. It’s the kind of glassware you’d love to put on your registry and display.

Jono Pandolfi plates are a fine dining staple. They’re served at luxury restaurants across the country—you can even see them listed on the page of each glaze color. So if you like to get experimental in the kitchen and want your dishes to look Michelin star, you can literally use what the pros use.

The brand that ushered in a ceramic cookware takeover also has beautiful ceramic tableware. They have dinnerware bundles that are easy to build on over time, perfect for cozy brunches even in tiny apartment kitchens to feel a little fancier. It’s a starter set that doesn’t feel like it’s from the bargain bin.

#6794. Is Astrology Right or Left?

Like many aspects of American life, astrology is experiencing a political reckoning

In 1988, a former aide to President Reagan alleged that Nancy Reagan relied heavily on the astrologer Joan Quigley to guide key moments in her husband’s presidency. The Reagans denied that characterization, but the image of an easily swayed Nancy Reagan using some irrational force to push through Reagan’s policy decisions entered general pop culture, fueled in part by the misogyny of the political press toward most women in politics, left or right, in the 1980s.

The story of Joan Quigley and Nancy Reagan is part of a larger history of people turning to the stars to help explain, and potentially influence, political events. This is an impulse that doesn’t belong to any particular political ideology. But in current pop culture, astrology is often used as shorthand for liberal or leftist worldviews. Leading up to the 2024 U.S. presidential election, some online astrologers confidently celebrated a potential Harris win. Their despair over the actual outcome was reported in numerous outlets. It seemed to confirm the stereotype of who follows astrology: millennials who are deeply disturbed by the current state of the world.

Today, astrology, like weightlifting, holistic medicine, and organic farming, has become less a hobby and more a political signifier. So many practices that used to be read as lifestyle choices—tracking your macros, for instance, or taking up ceramics—have, in our hyperpolarized culture, become stand-ins for a person’s political leanings. But what’s curious about astrology is that it reads as liberal-coded to those outside the field and potentially conservative to those inside it.

In a 2022 piece titled “Astrology and the Search for Meaning” in The American Conservative, Bradford Tuckfield attributed the rise in astrology to the fact that cultural discourse had “damage[d] or destroy[ed] the …positive myths that used to animate people,” adding, “The patriotic idea of America as a great nation has been undermined by many efforts for decades, the most notable recent example being the ‘1619 Project.’ ” For conservatives like Tuckfield, astrology is an attempt to fill a vacuum left in the American psyche. The absence of religion and the demand that citizens grapple with uncomfortable historic truths supposedly left young people ideologically lost. “The myth of social justice seems to have absorbed much of the energy” of young people, Tuckfield claimed. And astrology is just like that “myth.” For conservatives, then, astrology is just one of many cultural challenges to a homogeneous culture built on the myth of the hero. Astrology, in their reading, leads to challenges to the status quo, which is, by definition, the antithesis of conservatism.

But that’s how outsiders view the field. Astrology also has its own organizational bodies that strive to define the discipline and often are administered by older, more politically and culturally conservative practitioners. But those groups’ influence over astrology has waned as it has become more accessible through the internet. Once-esoteric knowledge is now widely distilled through TikToks and live streams, and this has led to a political divide for many astrologers.

“To be able to study astrology historically has been a very privileged thing to do. And so it’s been dominated by white folks,” explains the astrologer Chani Nicholas. Nicholas is a popular astrologer who trained with those unaffiliated with traditional astrology organizations. “There’s very explicit reasons why I’ve not invested a tremendous amount of time in general astrology communities, even though I am massively indebted to the community and I have a deep appreciation for it,” she says.

Nicholas’s brand of astrology makes explicit connections between planetary transits and real-world political events. She directly cites progressive and leftist political goals in the context of astrology. Nicholas traces the political divide in astrology to post-pandemic America and links it to the same divide seen in alternative health spaces and yoga practices, where many practitioners became enthralled with MAGA and MAHA politics while others did not.

Before the pandemic, organized astrology was dominated by “a group of white people that are mostly able-bodied, mostly heterosexual, mostly cisgendered, that have never had to confront their own racism, sexism, homophobia, transphobia,” she notes. “In the pandemic, a lot of millennials started taking classes with a lot of establishment teachers, and a lot of the racism started to come out.… Millennials were like, ‘I’m not going to sit here and have you say that. You can’t say that.’ And then the more establishment teachers were like, ‘Why are you coming for me?’ ”

This tension between a more conservative old guard in astrology and a younger group of practitioners came to a head this summer when Stephen Forrest, an older practitioner of astrology, appeared on the Lila Astrology podcast. In the episode, he spoke about a satirical character he had once employed to make fun of racism, using the N-word in doing so. Forrest also brought up white-supremacist beliefs he had previously held on the inferiority of Black people’s intelligence; claimed to be related to Nathan Forrester, the founder of the KKK; and repeated the debunked myth that the KKK originally formed as a charity organization and not a terrorist one.

In response, younger astrology adherents asked that Forrest be removed as a speaker from an upcoming conference. Other older practitioners who were more deeply invested in astrology organizations cautioned against “cancellation.” Chani Nicholas, who was scheduled to appear at a conference that was also planning to host Forrest, pulled out of her appearance.

“Astrology attracts all these different types of people. It’s fascinating, but it doesn’t mean that because you’re interested in the human condition that you’re actually interested in interrogating your own biases and your own way of enacting violence on behalf of the system,” Nicholas says.

Astrology organizations, like many institutions currently, are caught between a younger generation, which is asking an older one to abide by the values of fairness and universal acceptance many purport, and older generations that are refusing to see the contradictions and biases in many of their organizations’ stances.

“There was no shared reality about where we had been working,” says Nicholas. “This younger generation of people that were really trying to understand identity and politics and location and power and privilege started to make this robust astrology community [of their own] and really push back against a lot of stuff that had just been existing unchecked for decades.”

Samuel F. Reynolds is an African American astrologer who, for more than 35 years, has chosen to work within astrological organizations. He originally came to astrology as a skeptic. One of the biases he pushes against is the tendency to ground all interpretations in Western culture. “When Uranus went into Libra, an older Western astrologer might say to you, ‘Oh yeah, that led to a time where there was an acceleration of divorces and where people started to gain more power related to marriage,’” Reynolds explains. “When they say things like that, I’m like, ‘Oh, okay, did that happen in Africa? And did that happen in India? Was that true in most of Asia?’ And the data actually is no, it didn’t happen during that same time frame.… It does bring up a philosophical question of what are we talking about and what are we looking at? Because the framework by which we talk about humanity is not quite as vast as astrologers like to believe when they talk about it. That’s the truth of it. You’re talking about the people who are like you or in some way who are adjacent to you.”

He remembers astrology conferences 20 years ago where “every other talk, someone would bring up the charts of the Beatles, Princess Di.” Astrology is an art of interpretation, which rests on the practitioner’s frame of reference. The limited references at conferences spoke to “the insularity in general of white people,” Reynolds says. He does note, though, that at a conference recently, he saw someone comparing the chart of Toni Morrison with Agatha Christie. “Why? I’m not exactly sure,” he jokes.

All of this conflict is happening in the wider context of big cultural and political shifts and the crumbling of any sort of authority or governing bodies. In extreme instability, the desire to understand why everything is changing makes astrology even more attractive. And astrology offers a lens for many to understand themselves. You may not be able to name why you have anxiety or why you tend to have chaotic romantic relationships or why you have trouble forming deep community bonds, but your planetary signature (and those of your friends and lovers) can provide you with something that looks like an answer.

But Nicholas cautions against giving too much sway to the question of transcendence and self-interrogation that astrology can sometimes provide. “I really want to talk about how we can build power and be effective now,” she says. “I don’t want to be out in outer space. I actually really want to be on earth.”

“Rumi loves fashion. She picks out her own clothes,” Knowles said. “We recently went to Disneyland, and she put on her Mickey Mouse shorts. Then she wanted to put on a sparkly printed top which had the same colors. It actually looked really cute.” The moment impressed Knowles, who noted Rumi was just 5 at the time.

#6791. John Legend, Lynn Nottage, and Whitney White on Reclaiming an American Classic From a Black Perspective

The three artists have teamed up on a musical adaptation of Imitation of Life

In 2020, against the dual backdrops of the isolating Covid-19 lockdown and the righteous fury of Black Lives Matter protests erupting across the country, John Legend and Lynn Nottage began exchanging messages. Though the EGOT-achieving musician and the two-time Pulitzer Prize–winning playwright couldn’t meet in person, the pair quietly became collaborators on a long-gestating project, now finally reaching the stage: a musical adaptation of Imitation of Life.

For nearly a century, Imitation of Life—which began as a 1933 best-selling novel by Fannie Hurst, became a John M. Stahl melodrama the following year, then was adapted for the screen again by Douglas Sirk in 1959—has been a cinematic fixture in Black American households, though not an uncontroversial one. Stahl’s iconic telling centers on Bea Pullman, a white widow and single mother, who takes in Delilah, a Black single mother, as a maid in exchange for housing her and her light-skinned daughter, Peola (Sarah Jane in Sirk’s version). When Bea boxes Delilah’s family pancake recipe and puts a caricature of her likeness on the package—an unmistakable nod to Aunt Jemima—she becomes a wealthy businesswoman. But Delilah, content simply to care for Bea and her daughter, turns down any share of the money. Meanwhile, Peola, seduced by her adjacency to whiteness, wrenchingly disavows her mother to pursue a life of passing.

As Sterling Brown’s scathing and influential 1935 review for Opportunity: A Journal of Negro Life made plain, the novel and film are mired in racist tropes. Yet despite its flaws, the story has continued capturing Black audiences, perhaps for the complex humanity clearly latent within Delilah’s and Peola’s characters. Now the story is being retold in a limited-run production opening November 21 at the Shed in New York, this time with a fully realized Delilah as the lead and an all-Black chief creative team at its core. Nottage wrote the book, while Legend—who also recently released his 11th studio album, Muse—composed the entire score. Whitney White, of Jaja’s African Hair Braiding and Liberation, directs, with Adrienne Warren starring as Delilah and Jessica Vosk as Bea. Leading up to rehearsals for the show, Nottage, Legend, and White—so in demand that she was pulled into a production meeting midconversation—took time to discuss their own relationships to Imitation of Life, their experience working together, and why the story’s themes remain as relevant as ever.

Lynn Nottage: I come from a generation where both films, Imitation of Life from the 1930s and 1950s, were fairly ubiquitous on television. I ended up seeing the films probably once or twice a year and found myself in sort of a tense, love-hate relationship with Imitation of Life, in that I was frustrated by the treatment of Delilah and Peola in 1934, then once again by Delilah and Sarah Jane in the 1950s. And yet I was fascinated by the racial politics and this young woman who had a really angsty relationship with her own identity. So I felt, when I was invited to work on this, that I had an opportunity to delve deeper into the character of Delilah and right some wrongs. She is so fascinating; she’s a mother, she’s an entrepreneur, she’s a woman who defies the expectations of the time. And yet throughout history, she has not been given her just deserts.

John Legend: I didn’t grow up knowing this film, but my parents and their generation know it very well. So when I told them that I was involved in this musical, they were so excited. And it made me realize how important this work was in our culture. But like Lynn, it was a fun challenge to reimagine the storytelling, where Delilah was centered, her emotionality was centered, her humanity was centered, and Peola’s humanity was centered as well.

And then, of course, I’ve never written a musical before. So part of the joy and educational experience that I’ve gotten from doing this is working with people who have so much brilliance and experience and understanding of a medium that I didn’t bring to the table. It was the perfect introduction, for me, to writing a musical. It’s been such a welcoming and edifying way to enter into this new branch of my career.

Nottage: John is a natural in this medium. It’s almost as if he had gotten a master’s degree in musical-theater writing. I think that, John, you have so many musicals in you and the world is going to be blown away.

Legend: Thank you. It really did make me think, why hadn’t I done this before?

Whitney White: For me, I was raised by a single mom, and she raised me at great personal cost—spiritual cost—in Chicago, on her own. So the 1930s film, I oddly was more familiar with it because I liked it more. I would sit with my grandmother when she was having her coffee, like, at whatever-o’clock in the morning, because neither of us would ever sleep. And for some reason, I attached myself to the 1930s film. The visuals of that film are so iconic. It’s like the foundation of Americana as it exists in global culture is visualized in that film. But also the story of Delilah echoes in so many ways. It’s almost like touching a stove that’s too hot for me. Because I’ve watched, my whole life, the world not see my mother in this way: her brilliance, her intelligence, her spunk, her grit. So I just knew that I could bring something to it. But I also knew that John and Lynn were going to give that character the eulogy and the eternal life that she’s always deserved.

Legend: Yeah, I think because of Lynn’s perspective and storytelling, we see other sides of Delilah. We see her being romantic. We see her being a brilliant businessperson, making a way out of no way—something that a lot of our ancestors, especially, had to do. They did what they had to do to realize the American dream.

Nottage: At the core, this is a story about the American dream, with all of its contradictions and complications. It’s a story about motherhood; it’s a story about friendship, but it’s also a story about passing. It’s what we are forced to do to negotiate and survive in a world that is sexist, in a world that is racist. Why, in America, do we constantly have to make compromises in order to reach for the things that we desire? Those sacrifices, most often, people of color have to make, women have to make. We are often asked to reshape our identities to fit into some sort of template that’s not necessarily comfortable for the shapes and forms of our bodies and our ideas and our identities. I think that’s why we keep coming back to this story, because the struggle remains real.

White: And I think, in the midst of having to constantly reshape our identities and bodies as Black folk, at the same time, we got the goods. We make culture. Here you have this character who saves this business, and what does she make? Delicious food that is worth millions of dollars. So while we’re forced to be at the bottom of the totem pole, we also make the goods that make capitalism work. That’s something I’ve really been sitting with: We’re so often plundered and taken from the most, and yet we are the foundation of capitalism.

Nottage: Can I ask you, Whitney, you’re looking particularly luminous today. Where are you going?

White: Girl, I’m sweaty. I just walked my child [to school]. My child is very heavy. I have this giant toddler; maybe he’s going to be LeBron. He didn’t want to walk to school, so he made me push him in his stroller.

Legend: I met your husband last week, so I see where he got it from.

Nottage: I totally digress, but I was like, “Look at you, you have, like, a rosy green glow.” [Laughs]

White: [Laughs] That beat that just happened is so beautiful because it’s so indicative of the show. Like, we’re talking about this heavy stuff, and then we all have this beautiful laugh. When I read the text and hear the music, it’s like I go through 10 cycles of emotions. I laugh, I cry, I fall in love, then I laugh and cry and fall in love again.

Legend: One of my favorite things from the workshops is watching the music come to life. You have all these amazing performers embodying all the things that I wrote in my little piano room at home.

Nottage: Theater is often so difficult and fraught to make because it is collaborative. I think one of the things that’s been really lovely about this process is the ease with which we, as collaborators, are capable of having conversations, because there’s so much mutual respect and appreciation. The bottom line in making a musical is that trust is necessary. It’s a tremendous leap of faith.

White [as she is pulled into production meeting]: I just want to add one last thing. Oh God, I don’t want to get too emotional. Never in my wildest dreams did I imagine that I would be sitting here with two of my favorite living artists. When I started in this business, this kind of makeup for a musical team was impossible. I can’t tell you how much pride and excitement that brings me, to be working alongside John and Lynn.

Nottage: Thank you. We love you, Whitney.

White: I’m going to go lovingly yell at people—lovingly, lovingly. Bye.

Legend: Our writing process started—we were meeting during Covid, so a lot of our first interactions were on Zoom. Lynn would send me pages with story dialogue and possible lyrical ideas. I would sit in my piano room and sketch out an idea with maybe a couple lyrics, but mostly just mumbling musical ideas around the theme.

Legend: One of the things that happens more in musical theater than I’m used to is the revision process and the workshopping process. … I’ve definitely done more revision in this show than I’ve ever done in my pop-music career.

Nottage: John, do you remember one of the very first conversations that we had? I said, “We’re probably going to write that opening-number song at least 15 times.” You’re like, “Oh, I don’t think so.” Here we are!

Legend: Here we are. We’ve rewritten it multiple times. [Laughs] I don’t have an extensive knowledge bank of musicals that I’ve seen and been a part of. So it was interesting, thinking about how to write for one, because part of it was like, “Well, let me just bring my own musical experience in the church and popular music and, you know, singing in a jazz a cappella group and singing show tunes in the choir in high school.” I just brought my own experience and said, “Let me just write the songs that I can be proud of and that I believe will work.” And then Lynn really taught me how to incorporate the storytelling into the songs.

Nottage: It’s so interesting to hear you say that, John, because I think one of the things that made the process so smooth is that you are a natural-born storyteller. There’s something that you understand instinctively and fundamentally about dramaturgy that I didn’t have to explain to you. I would venture to say that perhaps musicals are the hardest form of theater-making just because it is so layered.

But I also want to circle back to something that you said earlier, John, about the beginning of the process. It was Covid, and we were all thinking and making in isolation. There was something really lovely about reaching across the country to John and having these conversations about a book, even though we didn’t really, at that point, know each other. We spent a lot of time talking about ideas. It was absolutely thrilling. When I got that first mumble, I was like, “Oh my God, it’s John Legend mumbling, and it still sounds beautiful.” It was a song that we never, ultimately, ended up using. I think that eventually you should do an album of the outtakes.

Nottage: I just want to add, because we’ve been talking about some heavy themes, that the musical is not delivering spinach. The musical embodies all of the complexities of a beautiful woman like Delilah, a beautiful woman like Bea, a beautiful woman like Peola. It really is, in some ways, a celebration. It is a love letter. And it is a gift to be able to be in dialogue with these women.

#6789. A You’ve Got Mail Reboot Is in the Works—Here’s What We Know So Far

Clueless, 13 Going on 30, The Families Stone, and now, You’ve Got Mail—Nora Ephron’s 1998 classic is the latest beloved title to receive a reboot for the modern era. Will the film’s defining emails be exchanged for Instagram and TikTok DMs? And better yet, will Meg Ryan and Tom Hanks be involved in the 2020s version of the film? Plot details are incredibly sparse, but here’s what we know about the You’ve Got Mail reboot so far.

Details are currently under wraps, but it will likely not follow the original movie’s plot exactly. “Rather than a remake of the original 1998 film starring Tom Hanks and Meg Ryan, the new film will follow a new story,” The Hollywood Reporter revealed.

The original film, based on Miklós László’s 1937 Hungarian play Parfumerie, follows independent bookstore owner Kathleen Kelly (Ryan) and a corporate bookshop magnate Joe Fox (Hanks) as they fall in love anonymously through the internet, but they have no knowledge that he’s trying to put her out of business.

Jason Orley (Close Personal Friends, Bupkis, and I Want You Back) is directing and writing the current draft of the project, based on a screenplay from Deborah Kaplan and Harry Elfont. The original film’s producers, Neal Moritz and Tania Landau, are returning for the reboot.

Ryan and Hanks aren’t currently attached to the You’ve Got Mail reboot, but most details of the movie are not public yet. (Fingers crossed.)

The studio has not shared any details on which stars will be featured in the project. Fans will have to stay tuned for further details.

#6788. 3 Major Awards Shows Won’t Nominate Amanda Seyfried for 'You Can See Everything'

Awards season is almost upon us, which means celebrities are about to start campaigning hard—including Amanda Seyfried, whose brilliant performance as Elizabeth Holmes in You Can See Everything has earned a ton of Best Supporting Actress buzz.

While Variety previously reported that Amanda can be nominated for a Best Supporting Actress Oscar, apparently there are three major awards she will be ineligible for.

Per The Hollywood Reporter, Amanda is unable to be nominated for a Golden Globe, a BAFTA, or an Actor award due to her performance technically being in a documentary—though apparently SAG-AFTRA has told A24 they can appeal the decision.

Amanda told THR that she fully views her participation in the film as a performance, saying “The reenactments were definitely a challenge. But it’s not like I ever considered that I would be up for any kind of accolade. I understand the politics and the luck and the timing of awards stuff. I don’t really know if we can fully consider it a doc—it’s very genre-bending.”

Amanda added that she’s “fully present” for the awards season campaign, noting that “the amount of events and the amount of presence you need to have when campaigning for these things is pretty extreme—you saw me go through it last year—but it’s still all very positive because getting a seat at the table is the thing.”

She went on to explain that “A campaign is a lot. It’s time-consuming. It takes all your energy. And I’m totally willing to do it. A24 is great. They’re excited, and I’m very proud of the film, so I’m fully ready to engage in that. And I love these filmmakers! I had a great time with this tiny crew. And I think because [the experience] is so rare, and it will never happen again, I’m doubled-down on it.”

#6787. Ariana Grande and Ricky Álvarez Go Red-Carpet Official—at Last!

The pair walked the red carpet arm-in-arm for the world premiere of Focker-in-Law

Things have been moving quickly these past few months for Ariana Grande and Ricky Álvarez. As the story goes, the pair first met over a decade ago, when Álvarez served as a backup dancer on Grande’s Honeymoon Tour. They briefly dated at the time, and made some public appearances together, but broke up by the summer of 2016. Grande even referenced the dalliance in her hit song “thank u, next” (“Wrote some songs about Ricky / Now I listen and laugh”). Flash forward to this past summer, and the pair seemingly had a strong reconnection, being spotted on a number of dates and posing for PDA-filled Instagram posts. Grande even got a new tattoo that winks to the dancer. Now, they’ve just made their red-carpet debut.

Although Grande stepped away from the spotlight for a few months following the end of her Eternal Sunshine Tour on September 1, she made her grand return on Wednesday night to promote her new comedy film, Focker-in-Law, which hits theaters on November 25. And she did so with the support of Álvarez, who walked the red carpet with her at the film’s world premiere at the Beacon Theatre in New York City.

For the occasion, Grande—who was styled by Law Roach—wore a vintage, ’90s-era dress from Badgley Mischka, sourced from Jellybean Vintage.

The gray-and-white plaid design featured a scoop-neck bodice held up by impossibly-thin spaghetti straps, while the A-line skirt flared out in voluminous ruffles.

For a romantic finish, the dress was embroidered with tiny white florals that dotted the neckline, which Grande complemented with a bright-white ribbon holding up her hair bun. She accessorized with silver hoop earrings.

As for Álvarez, he chose a classic white button-up layered underneath an open black blazer and paired with rolled-up, dark-wash jeans, a chunky black belt, and brown loafers.

The lovebirds first sparked reconciliation rumors this past June, when they were spotted together in Austin, Texas, following one of Grande’s Eternal Sunshine shows. Then, Grande essentially confirmed their romance when she began changing the lyrics to “thank u, next” during her concerts.

At one show, she sang: “Wrote some songs about Ricky / I know he’s still got my back,” while at another she crooned: “Wrote some songs about Ricky / We always find our way back.”

But it was the change she made on the final stop of the tour that really seemed to indicate where the couple is at, when Grande sang: “Wrote some songs about Ricky / I’m tryna make him my last.” Now, that’s romantic.

#6786. This Historic Napa Valley Estate Features 18th-Century Charm, Fig Trees, and a Luxe Spa

Preserving the past while readying the property for a new chapter was the goal.

At Vine & Fig, each moment takes on a bit of everyday grandeur, spurred on by the sun-drenched warmth and crisp air of Napa County. The aptly named estate, set among vineyards, olive groves, and fig trees in St. Helena, California, has always had the makings of a particularly good gathering place; it just needed a little help catching up with the times.

Enter designer Jaime Rummerfield, who spent four years transforming the 19th-century property into a 10,500-square-foot retreat that feels both comfortable and reverential to its past. The undertaking was hardly a straightforward process. Between fires, floods, and the delicate logistics of preserving the estate’s historic architecture and geothermal streams, Rummerfield had to tackle the renovation with equal parts imagination and restraint. “You cannot approach a place like this as a blank canvas,” she explains. “Understanding what is already there becomes part of the design narrative.”

Fortunately, she had a willing creative partner in homeowner Olivia Marciano, whose background as artistic director of the Marciano Art Foundation brought an especially discerning perspective to the process. The two spent hours considering materials, palettes, and custom details, often allowing a single piece to inform the direction of an entire room. The resulting interiors are richly layered yet approachably livable: antique-style cabinetry with exposed hinges, hand-crafted furnishings, expressive stone, and artwork that lends the rooms a sense of collected history. “Everything shouldn’t feel as though it arrived on the same truck,” Rummerfield says.

Given that this is Napa Valley, it goes without saying that the perks of the property extend well beyond its walls. A chef’s kitchen opens onto the surrounding gardens, while the historic pool house was reimagined as an immersive hammam, complete with Moroccan mosaics, Taj Mahal stone, and copper hot and cold plunge tubs. But for Rummerfield, the property’s greatest luxury isn’t any single amenity, but the new chapter unfolding around it. “There are vineyards, olive groves, gardens, food being prepared, and people coming together,” she says. "The interiors are the stage and participate in all of that.” And at Vine & Fig, there's always room for one more act.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Jaime RummerfieldBuilder: JMA Uncommon BuildersArchitect: 4C Design GroupLandscape Architect: Surfacedesign, Inc Location: St. Helena, CaliforniaThe Space: A six-bedroom, ten-bathroom home across 10,500 square feet

Designed to accommodate everything from quiet family dinners to spirited parties, the kitchen embraces the property’s farm-to-table ethos. Antique-style cabinetry with exposed butt hinges lends the space a sense of history, while a white oak island, stone backsplash, and French trestle table keep things grounded. Beyond the windows, views of the mountains and gardens reinforce the connection between the home and its surroundings.

“We wanted the practical decisions and the decorative decisions to support one another,” adds Rummerfield. “The cabinetry details, hidden technology, ample storage, textiles, and connection to the outdoors keep it feeling like a room in a home. It is a chef’s delight, but it is also a very welcoming heart of the house.”

“We wanted this multi-use kitchen to have a personality appropriate to its setting while still feeling connected to the wider property, hammam, and pool,” says Rummerfield of this bonus cook space. “The proportions and material relationships become especially important. It ended up being one of our favorite spaces—the rich chocolate zellige tile back splash really made it unique and divine.”

Few amenities feel more appropriate to a Napa Valley estate than a dedicated wine cave. Here, Rummerfield let the architecture and natural materials establish the atmosphere, incorporating honed black soapstone countertops to create a space that feels equally suited to storing prized bottles and sharing a favorite vintage with friends.

Rummerfield bypassed a rigid furniture arrangement in favor of several intimate seating areas, creating a room that feels equally suited to lively conversation and afternoon lounging. “I wanted the living room to have a sense of occasion while remaining completely comfortable,” she says.

To that end, a generously proportioned, low-slung sectional offers a particularly inviting place to land, while custom cabinetry conceals modern technology, including a drop-down projector screen, without interrupting the room’s collected aesthetic.

“Passageways are wonderful places to create a moment of discovery,” Rummerfield says of the dramatic entryway and curved staircase, which features the client’s art colleciton. “I want these spaces to feel as personal, stylish and welcoming as the rooms they connect to.”

An arched ceiling and soft lighting sets the tone for the journey into the property’s reimagined pool house, where Rummerfield wanted to create “a completely different experience” within the property. “The hammam is about slowing down and enjoying the beauty of the materials,” she adds. “The barrel ceiling, Moroccan mosaic, and Taj Mahal stone give the space a wonderful sense of intimacy and ritual.”

“Powder rooms offer a lovely opportunity for personalization; the relationship between the vanity, sink, mirror, and light can give the room a distinct personality,” says Rummerfield. In here, she sources a Turkish sink from Olive Ateliers, incorporated a 1940’s glass sconce, inlaid an antique mirror, and brought the terra cotta paver floors from outside in. “Each element is deliberately chosen and work beautifully together,” she adds.

Affectionately dubbed the “Strawberry Room” after the pattern on the BDDW chair, this bedroom offers comfort and relaxation in space. Rummerfield leaned into the room’s cozy proportions with a tactile Phillip Jeffries wallcovering, layered fabrics, and sculptural lighting by Ken Fulk for Urban Electric.

“I like a bathroom to feel considered as a room in its own right,” says Rummerfield. “The vanity, mirror, lighting, and tub should have a pleasing relationship, with the materials doing much of the decorative work. The atmosphere should feel quiet and indulgent.”

“I enjoy giving utility spaces some personality,” Rummerfield says of the laundry room, which she coated in Benjamin Moore’s Spring Valley, a fresh green that nods to the surrounding landscape. “Many people spend time in them, so the light, finishes, and everyday experience deserve attention.”

Jaime Rummerfield is a Los Angeles–based interior designer known for her richly layered spaces that balance timeless elegance with a distinct sense of personality. Her work celebrates craftsmanship, architectural character, and the art of collecting, combining vintage finds, custom furnishings, and expressive materials to create interiors that feel both sophisticated and lived-in.

Head-to-toe burgundy is the look of the day.

Sitting somewhere between cherry and oxblood, burgundy takes its name from the red wines of France’s Burgundy region (what’s not to love?). The color has infiltrated every corner of fall wardrobes in recent seasons, from standout leather and liquid satin to plush knitwear. #6784. There’s Major New Evidence That Robert Pattinson Is the Next James Bond

Hardly a day goes by without a report dropping that someone’s cousin’s friend’s brother overheard someone else’s cousin’s friend’s brother at a bar say that [insert hot British actor here] is the next James Bond. And so far, Amazon MGM and director Denis Villeneuve have not confirmed one. single. thing.

However, we do know that several actors screen-tested for the iconic role, including Jack Lowden, Josh O’Connor, and—surprise!—Robert Pattinson. And according to Matthew Belloni in Puck News, there’s reason to think Robert is favored to become the next 007.

Turns out all potential Bonds sign contracts for the film and its follow-ups—and three separate sources told Belloni that while Jack and Josh “were required to sign up for four Bonds,” Robert “was able to lower that commitment to three” and negotiate himself “a whole lot more money than the others.”

So, what does that mean? Yet more sources explain that since Robert’s “test deal was so difficult to close,” people close to the situation are “wondering why the filmmakers would bother if they didn’t really want him.” Innnnnteresting!

There’s definitely some discourse about whether or not Bond should be an established household name (traditionally, he isn’t), with former casting director Debbie McWilliams—who cast the last three Bonds—telling The Independent, “It is absolutely essential that he retains a total enigma. I don’t want to see any of them as Bond because we now know so much about them. We want to know as little about them personally as possible, because that what spies are. We don’t need to know where he goes shopping or who his parents are, or where he lives. We never want to see him at home. And a vital element of the whole thing is his job description. He’s licensed to kill, and we have to believe that he can do that. If you don’t, then you’ve lost the audience.”

Casting Robert Pattinson definitely challenges that approach, but we’ll see!

#6781. The Sisters Behind Dannijo Are Making Sports Merch Cool

DannijoPro was built on the idea that female fans deserve game-day gear they actually want to wear.

When Knicks frenzy overtook New York City this past summer during the team’s historic championship run, women fans—both long-suffering devotees and bandwagoners alike—went looking for something to wear. And in a city known for its fashion sense and individuality, the same old generic logo shirts and jerseys simply would not do.

DannijoPro answered the call. Seemingly overnight, the brand’s cropped blue satin bombers, bedazzled denim jackets with crochet collars, and artfully embroidered vintage sports tees took over Instagram, with celebrities like Brooke Shields, cool girls, and Ali Brunson, wife of star point guard Jalen Brunson, and Jordyn Woods, who wed center Karl-Anthony Towns shortly after he won the chip, photographed wearing DannijoPro. Knicks royalty Spike Lee even wore a DannijoPro custom jacket courtside at Game 5.

Danielle and Jodie (Danni + Jo, get it?) Snyder, the sisters and co-founders behind the bespoke fanwear company (a sporty spinoff of their original jewelry and fashion brand, Dannijo), say they were just happy to be along for the historic ride. “No one could have predicted the Knicks were going to go as far as they did, but that was amazing for us,” Jodie says of the win that ended a 53-year title drought. “People loved what we were doing.” Their Knicks finals shirts sold out three times; all 800 satin bombers (priced at $395) sold out, too. Overall, the sisters tell me their sales for 2026 are up 200 percent from last year. “I’ve never seen anything like it in my life,” says Danielle, who, along with Jodie, lived in NYC for over a decade and visited during the finals. “The energy was electric and there was such community. People were buying merch because it was so much more than a garment. It’s a piece of history. It’s a relic now.”

The Knicks run may have fueled their virality, but DannijoPro’s popularity also stems from the fact that it is serving an audience that has been ignored for too long by companies that thought that, when designing sports merch for women, it was enough to “pink it and shrink it.” And although 75 percent of women identify as fans of one or more sports, and women’s sports merch is estimated to be a $4 billion market, still a quarter of female fans say they can’t find styles they like. “It’s crazy that we’re in 2026 and you can go to a game and not find anything you want to buy as a female fan,” Jodie says.

Three months after the Knicks took home the trophy, I reach the sisters as they are gearing up for the launch of their new NBA collection for the 2026-27 season and a number of other new partnerships that have come in the wake of their viral turn, including one rolling out this month with their hometown team, the Jacksonville Jaguars—DannijoPro’s first NFL collab. Danielle is in the brand’s atelier in Tiburon in Marin County, California; as we chat on Zoom, I see people hand-stitching various garments in the background. Jodie joins the call from her car in Jacksonville, Florida, where she heads up the company’s retail and business strategy.

The sisters, who are three years apart, grew up as close as could be in Jacksonville. They’ve been creative, crafty, and entrepreneurial from a young age. Their mom, a mental health counselor, didn’t have pierced ears, but she did have a killer clip-on earring collection, and the girls were obsessed with it. In middle school, Jodie took an interest in making jewelry of her own, and Danielle, wanting to do everything her big sister did, followed right along.

Their dad is a cardiologist, and they taught themselves how to do wire work using his medical tools. Soon, they were collaborating on intricately beaded designs and selling them in local stores. “We’d see kids around school wearing our handmade designs and they didn’t even know it was us making them,” Danielle says. “What we were doing was really fresh and cool. Girls and women could take our accessories and update a white tee and jeans, or anything basic, and make it their own. That was our first taste of using style to individualize and to tell stories and we caught the bug.”

They got their big break a few years later when Danielle was in middle school. Neiman Marcus bought 150 of their turquoise nugget necklaces. “I remember staying up until 3 or 4 in the morning cranking them out,” Danielle says. “And I had an exam the next morning.” The Snyders continued to balance school and their design ambitions. Years later, when Jodie was at the University of Florida studying business and public relations and Danielle at Vanderbilt, where she majored in psychology and Spanish, Jodie would fly up and they would do trunk shows at sororities on campus. “We were always making stuff for wherever we were in life,” Jodie explains. “And everything we do is rooted in sisterhood and wanting to be together, wanting to express ourselves,” Danielle adds.

Jodie graduated from college in 2004 and moved to New York City, with Danielle following in 2007. Dannijo was founded the following year and, like the Snyders’ childhood company, quickly became known for its statement jewelry pieces. Their collection was sold in Bergdorf Goodman and Henri Bendel; characters on Gossip Girl and anchors on the Today show wore their necklaces; Beyoncé and Kim Kardashian did too (purchasing them themselves). “I remember the saleswoman calling us and saying, ‘You’re never going to believe who bought the Ruth Celia necklace,’” Danielle says. It was Queen B, who went on to wear it at Coachella, courtside at NBA games, and on a trip with her husband, Jay-Z, in Europe. “So we started getting press before we even really knew what PR was.”

They soon expanded into fashion, designing the “perfect silk slip dress” modeled after Kate Moss in the ’90s. When Jodie’s daughter was 3, she went to a tie-dye party and the next thing they knew, Dannijo was selling tie-dyed hoodies. They styled it over a slip dress and it went viral. “We sold thousands of tie-dye hoodies—it was insane,” Jodie says. “Basically, we just made things that we wanted to wear.” Collabs with Starbucks, Mercedes-Benz, and Swarovski followed.

In 2020, as the pandemic took hold in NYC, the sisters, like many residents, decamped for other parts of the country. Jodie moved back to Florida, while Danielle moved to the Bay Area. The move across the country was hard on Danielle, who was suffering from postpartum depression. “I was lost, lonely, and I didn’t have community,” she notes.

One day, her husband came home with Golden State tickets and told her they were going out. “That first Warriors game changed my life,” Danielle explains. “It changed my perspective. It got me excited again—I was excited to root for a team and to be a part of something.” They started attending games regularly and Danielle began creating her own designs to wear. “I started going through my husband’s closet and cutting up all of his old jerseys and making them into outfits for me,” she says. “My fix is always to be creative, so I was using my hands, doing stitch work, adding crystals, what have you.” Other female fans at the games began taking notice of her creative designs and asking where she had bought them. “Everyone was noticing because there was just nothing like it out there, so I called Jodie and I said, ‘This is really big.’” Soon, Dannijo’s sporty sister brand, DannijoPro, was born.

Launching a company focused on women fans was a “no-brainer,” Danielle says. “When I started making myself stuff to wear, that’s when I noticed what a crazy white space it was. Sports have never been more popular, but the product just had not caught up to that reality.” It took a year and a half to get an NBA license, but once they did, the first DannijoPro collection, which rolled out in 2024 at the Chase Center (where the Warriors play), quickly sold out.

As ever, the sisters are their own inspiration. Jodie has two children and Danielle has three. “Our worlds have changed,” Jodie says. “We’re both in T-shirts and sweatshirts; we’re not getting dressed up every day anymore.” As such, the DannijoPro items they make aren’t just for game day. They are for the 70 percent of women fans who say they’ve bought sports merch, while only 51 percent have bought tickets to attend an event. “We wanted to create items that people want to wear on and off the court or field,” Jodie says. “It's about making flattering silhouettes and clothes we all want to wear, and then applying them to the teams that we’re excited about.”

DannijoPro has three main products: officially licensed merch complete with the signature touches the designers have come to be known for, like crochet collars, hand-stitching, painting, and crystal work; “blanks” that can be customized with the help of its in-house artists (most orders come via DM); and vintage pieces that are reworked by hand.

As Jodie talks, Danielle runs around the atelier pulling out products from their new collections to show me. In August, they launched designs they made for avatars in the NBA 2K game. In September, they debuted a collab with Roger Federer’s Laver Cup tennis tournament. Their new NBA collection, called “Game Day 2.0,” rolled out last month, while their Jacksonville Jaguars line will go on sale on November 17. Future partnerships with other teams and leagues are in the works.

The sisters see their new company as “so much bigger than a fashion brand,” Danielle says. “We are reimagining fandom through a culture lens, and it’s about people. We’re living in a world of AI where everything’s like a bot. For us, it’s like, how do we bring really cool dynamic experiences in real life to people and create connections and community?”

“We think women fans should be celebrated in that there are products out there that speak to us,” Jodie adds. “It’s more than merch. I think it stands for so many other things. We can’t just accept that it’s okay to just slap logos on things, or pink it and shrink it, and women are just going to go with that. And at the end of the day, all these companies forget, but women have the buying power.”

#6779. 8 Popular Home Items From 20 Years Ago That Are Making a Surprising Comeback

They may be a blast from the past, but they’re about to be very now.

Trends come and go, but they almost always come back again. If there’s one thing we know about interior design, it’s that what feels old and outdated manages to become fresh and cool again in a few years’ time. Skeptics can easily be proved wrong with the resurgence of sunken living rooms, glass block walls, and even cushy sofas plucked right out of a Nora Ephron movie. And, as younger generations continue to be intrigued with the 1990s and early 2000s, they are turning to the era’s interiors for inspiration.

Well, sort of, as Maggie Vollrath of Provision Studios points out. “When we look back at certain interiors from the 2000s, there was such a commitment to achieving a particular look that every element in the room was chosen to reinforce it,” the Georgia designer says. “A home should feel like a collection of things you genuinely love, not as though you’ve walked onto a movie set designed to represent a particular style.”

When you cherry-pick trends and design details from the past, you’re able to create a home that honors the past without appearing stuck in the past. “The goal isn’t to make a home look like it belongs to another decade,” Vollrath says. “You want to bring back the beauty and character of those decorative details while creating something that feels authentic to both the house and its owners.”

Below, designers share trends from the past that the next generation of design lovers adore.

For older generations, a swivel chair might conjure memories of sitting in your family’s den, but designers promise these movin’, groovin’ seats have been revived for the 21st century. “Swivel barrel chairs defined early-2000s transitional interiors, usually in a bright flat polyester-blend upholstery,” explains Hallie Malitsky, principal designer of Hallie Rose Design in Brooklyn. “The version coming back today is a different experience entirely.”

Today’s iterations, she insists, are subtler. Cheap polyester has yielded to rich, tactile fabrics like wool, linen, and velvet. For a timely twist, she also encourages trading the fire engine red and butter yellow popular in the 2000s for burgundy and ochre. “Surround it with natural materials like wood, linen, and stone, and [your chair] will stop being the loudest thing in the room,” she recommends.

When Malitsky thinks of early aughts design, she immediately envisions the gigantic walnut media center that plagued many of her friends’ family rooms. “Dark wood cabinetry is a notorious culprit of bad 2000s design, but the wood wasn’t the issue,” she says. “It was the fact that there was so much of it.”

Now that dark wood tones are making a comeback, Malitsky says to use them sparingly. Two to three walnut or ebony pieces per room will make enough of a statement. And, no: Unlike the living rooms of yore, your furniture doesn’t have to match.

“What I love about their return is that we’re incorporating these pieces in a much more collected way,” shares Vollrath. “Rather than purchasing an entire matching furniture collection, we’re mixing periods and bringing in individual pieces that have character and history.”

The early aughts had lots of elaborate cabinetry, replete with corbels, substantial cornices, and beautifully detailed range hoods. As everyone—yes, even younger generations—continue to favor more traditional interiors, we’re seeing a renewed interest in ornamented cabinetry.

“There’s something really special about a kitchen that feels as though it could have been there for generations, or even reminds you of your grandmother’s kitchen,” Vollrath says. “Those decorative flourishes bring a sense of history, craftsmanship, and permanence that can be difficult to achieve with a more streamlined approach.”

But why stop there when you can add character to other rooms of the house?

“We’re seeing a broader interest in architectural detailing throughout the home, from wainscoting and wall paneling to ceiling beams and substantial moldings,” she adds. “Homeowners are becoming more interested in understanding these elements, considering them during renovations, and making them a priority in new construction.”

If the rise of analog interiors proves anything, it’s that even the best chatbot can’t beat excellent design. Instead of having a home covered in screens and smart systems, many are favoring more primitive setups that harken back to dial-up and CD-ROMs. For example, Fargo-based designer Lindsey Christie has been noticing (and loving) the return of landlines. “There's something wonderfully nostalgic about a beautiful telephone sitting on a bedside table or tucked into a thoughtfully designed corner of a home,” she says.

Of course, there’s plenty of fun to be had in a “dumb home.” “We have had several adult clients wanting to incorporate their hobby of Legos into their design aesthetic,” reveals Allison Smith of Gem + Clay Interiors in St. Augustine, Florida. “I definitely think this speaks to a desire to return to the analog, and that [old-school] element.”

“We always joke about the Tuscan kitchens from 2005, but they had some good things going on,” says Drew Michael Scott of Lone Fox. If you strip away the “bottles of preserved peppers and gaudy oil-rubbed bronze faucets,” the style was actually rocking a great color palette. Shades like brown, ochre, terracotta, and deep red bring a little bit of the great outdoors inside, plus they can pair nicely with today’s cozy interiors. But let’s keep the overly Italian influences in the past, shall we?

Today’s emerging design lovers are extra—just ask Scott, who has been noticing a considerable uptick in passementerie. “These intricate details were everywhere in the mid-2000s, before the world decided everything should be sleek and gray,” he says. “They’re becoming popular again, and I think they really evoke the attention to detail we saw at the turn of the century.”

Though yesteryear’s tassels, beading, and decorative trim are undeniably fun, using them requires a little bit of strategy. Since there’s a fine line between charming and kitschy, balance these louder, upholstered moments with pared-back wooden pieces.

Goodbye, panel-ready refrigerators and dishwashers: Katie Kiser says stainless steel appliances are so back. According to the Tennessee-based designer, the resurgence is a break from the picture-perfect kitchens older generations have pined over. “Gen-Z values practicality over perfection,” she explains. “I think it will show up in their interiors.” And why stop at appliances when you can bring that lived-in feel to the rest of the space? “They don’t hate clutter the way millennials do,” she adds. “We’ll see details like cookbooks in the kitchens and hall trees with tennis rackets on them.”

Though Scott hasn’t seen the Venetian Gold granite that ruled Y2K just yet, he’s noticing many homeowners are willing to get creative with their countertops.

“We've moved beyond using only Carrara marble in kitchens,” he explains.

Ditto for quartz, which some designers believe is on the outs. Instead, Scott says travertine is huge again and granite is poised to make the comeback. If you want to keep these materials from appearing dated, he encourages you to reach for stones with bolding veining and inclusions.

#6778. The “Brainy Heel” Is This Season’s Shoe MVP

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

They’ll make every outfit feel more thoughtful

Sometimes, you just want to dress for yourself. It’s not about man-repelling so much as just responding to your own needs: I want to feel good, look well-dressed, and be drawn to what I’m wearing—for me, not them.

Looking at the new fall/winter 2026 shoes, from the feathered hiking styles at Prada to the peaked-toe lace-up shoes at Jil Sander to the grown-up Mary Janes at Chanel, I found myself coming back to that thought. These are heels for me, not them: attractive, but not designed purely to attract; chic, but not flashy; and, dare I say it, made with the wearer’s comfort in mind.

They’re shoes that make you think—and make you wonder what the woman wearing them is thinking too. You’re intrigued by her sartorial choices, especially her heels. Brainy heels? Perhaps. But like the woman herself, maybe they don’t need a title. Just a formula.

As with any well-designed object, what makes these shoes appealing is the law of proportions—the relationship between shapes that creates a visual balance. And what makes them compelling is that there’s always something just a little off, something to make you look again or make you wonder why, something that opposes and attracts at the same time. We’ve written about the wrong-shoe theory, the comfort-first commuter shoe, and plenty of the season’s chicest styles. The brainy heel brings them all together in one slightly off-kilter package.

#6777. The Woven Tote Bag Trend Is Taking Over Runways and Workdays

Woven bags have officially reached icon status.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Is there any other bag that works as hard in real life as the tote bag? When it comes to being on duty and looking good, probably not. The humble tote has come in all iterations: lightweight cotton, supple leather, and suede. But more recently, the runways have been obsessed with making the oversize tote-style bags in luscious jumbo woven iterations. We saw them on the spring/summer 2027 runways at Dries Van Noten, Courrèges, Bottega Veneta, and Altuzarra. And then, labels like Bembien, Naghedi, and Dragon Diffusion are pumping out woven totes like there’s no tomorrow.

A big woven bag offers up texture and interest in a sea of ubiquitous canvas logo totes. It also gives you a bit of resilience so you don’t have to baby your bag on, say, a subway commute or a brisk morning walk where the risk of bumping into others is second nature. It is the working girl’s best friend. That’s just one of the reasons why fashion retailers have fallen head over heels for the style lately.

“Regardless of lifestyle, everyone needs a bag that carries a lot, and potentially the kitchen sink,” Ryan Kleman, Moda Operandi’s VP of buying, accessories, tells ELLE. The great news about a woven bag is that it also seamlessly goes with everything. Tone it down for a day at the office with a button-down top or a casual dress, or take it out for a nice dinner; a silk scarf tied about the strap can be the pop of color or embellishment you might crave for a more formal occasion.

In the case of some of these brands, the woven tote is literal fashion history. Bottega Veneta, for example, first introduced its signature “Intrecciato” woven leather technique in 1975. The fashion house’s spring/summer 2027 collection modernized the technique by applying it to enormous, hulking bags that swayed when worn over the shoulder. Paired with layered sweaters and matching leather belts, the bags felt like a decidedly fresh take on wearable artisanship by creative director Louise Trotter.

In the case of Dries Van Noten, one model carried an absolutely huge woven raffia-effect foldover clutch. Styled with a heather gray hoodie rolled up to reveal leather bubblegum-pink opera gloves with a biker-style cutout and buckle at the hand, the bag itself proved just how versatile a woven piece can be.

If you’re indeed thinking of upgrading your everyday work bag for a woven tote, it’s better to think bigger. “The oversized scale of the bags and the woven construction provide the perfect details to convert a chore into a fashion statement,” says Kleman. “At Moda, we have dubbed this trend the Big A** Bag and our customers are loving it.” This, too, is true: the bigger the weave, the better the carryall.

#6776. Here’s Everything We Know About ‘Campus Drivers’ Season 2 (and Which Couple It’ll Be About)

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Hello to everyone who watched all of Campus Drivers in one day and now urgently need to know what’s going on with season 2: We have answers about what comes next for Lois and Lane and their delightfully trope-y love story.

The first season of Campus Drivers saw Lois break up with her longtime boyfriend and have no choice but to move in with “campus bad boy” Lane in his ultra-fancy apartment. At first Lois can’t stand him, then they become fast friends, then there’s the whole “let’s make out in front of your ex to make him jealous” phase, and finally the season ends with Lane and Lois fully getting together...only for Lane’s mom to enroll him in a new school. Lois literally comes home to a bunch of movers and literally no information from her boyfriend, so yeah, obviously season 2 can’t come fast enough! Here’s what we know.

Not yet. Prime is likely in the “wait and see how it performs” stage of things, but the show is in their top 10, so a renewal seems pretty inevitable at this point.

Lane and Lois obviously have a lot of issues to resolve, but reminder: Campus Drivers is based on a book series. And if season 2 is based on book 2 (aka Crash Course), then a brand-new couple will take center stage: “college bad boy trying to change gears” Donovan and “romance-loving good girl” Carrie. Their general vibe? Let’s dust off the book description.“When Donovan, basketball star and notorious heartthrob of the Campus Drivers, realizes the damage his womanizing behavior has caused, he decides to become the perfect boyfriend. And who better to coach him than romance-obsessed, all-around good girl Carrie? Carrie wants nothing to do with Donovan. She’d much rather lose herself in the heroes from her romance novels. They’re uncomplicated and drama-free, just the way she likes it. If Donovan wants her help, he’ll have to endure a tough, no-nonsense crash course in romance. But one can’t just become a romance hero overnight—especially when reality refuses to stick to the script.”

Time to do some math. According to IMDb, Campus Drivers’ first season was filmed from October 2025 to January 2026. It then dropped in November. This is a pretty fast turnaround, so assuming the show is renewed and filming takes place in early 2027, we could be looking at a late 2027 or early 2028 release date. So, about a year from now (or a little longer).
